Franken FDK AAC

Franken FDK AAC is the Fraunhofer FDK AAC encoder (libfdk-aac 2.0.3, mstorsjo/fdk-aac) with the nu774/fdkaac 1.0.2 frontend, extended with switches that expose decisions FDK normally makes on its own: how stereo is coded, how the high band is rebuilt, where bands may be cut to zero, how transients are handled, how bits are distributed over time. It is meant for engineers who want to hear and measure what those decisions do, especially at the extremes: very low bitrates, forced wide bands, speech, streaming.

Author: Michał Dziwisz. Subject-matter consultant: Patryk Faliszewski. Built on open source: libfdk-aac (Fraunhofer IIS) and the nu774/fdkaac frontend.

Without any of the new switches the encoder produces exactly what stock FDK produces, byte for byte. Every switch is opt-in, and every one of them can also make things worse: they let you go where FDK's own tuning deliberately does not.

Download

Prebuilt Windows binaries are published as GitHub Releases and download without an account:

https://github.com/michaldziwisz/franken-fdk-aac/releases/latest

Beta versions (for example v1.4.0-beta.1) are published as pre-releases, so the link above still shows the last stable version; betas are on the full list: https://github.com/michaldziwisz/franken-fdk-aac/releases

franken-fdk-aac-x64-vX.Y.Z.zip is 64-bit, the x86 one is 32-bit. Each zip contains the static encoder (no DLLs) and the full documentation. The repository itself holds sources only; building is described at the end of this document.

First steps

All original frontend options work as before (-p, -b, -m, -w, -a, -s, -f, tagging); fdkaac-franken-x64.exe --help lists them together with the new ones.

  1. Encode once with --verbose and read what the encoder actually set up:

    fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 2 -b 128000 --verbose -o out.m4a in.wav

  2. Change one switch at a time and check in --verbose, under franken overrides applied, that it took effect.

  3. Listen, and where it matters, measure: decode both versions and compare. The tools in tools/ in the repository do this for holes, pre-echo and stereo error.

Recipes

Each of these is a tested starting point, not a preset to apply blindly.

# LC stereo at a low bitrate with a wide band: fewer holes, less pre-echo
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 2 -b 32000 -w 17000 --hole-ladder 2 --avoid-holes 1 --window-shape 1 -o out.m4a in.wav

# The same at 40 kbps, with a calmer attack detector for percussive material
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 2 -b 40000 -w 18000 --hole-ladder 2 --avoid-holes 1 --window-shape 1 --attack-decay 0.9 -o out.m4a in.wav

# More stereo width at the expense of the centre
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 2 -b 96000 --side-bias 6 -o out.m4a in.wav

# Quasi-VBR around 128 kbps: CBR engine with room to breathe
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 2 -b 128000 --peak-bitrate 160000 --vbr-reservoir 6000 -o out.m4a in.wav

# HE-AAC with a softer, less metallic high band
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 5 -b 32000 --sbr-softness 4 -o out.m4a in.wav

# HE-AAC v2 with a wider core than FDK picks
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 29 -b 48000 --core-cutoff 7500 -o out.m4a in.wav

# Stream that a listener tuning in decodes with SBR at once
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 5 -b 48000 --sbr-header-period 1 -f 2 -o out.aac in.wav

# AAC-LD with a wider core and more TNS against pre-echo (LD needs LOAS, not ADTS)
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 23 -b 64000 --core-cutoff 12000 --tns-thresh 0.7 -f 10 -o out.loas in.wav

# Extremely low HE-AAC v2: 8000 bps stereo
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 29 -b 8000 --unlock-bitrate -o out.m4a in48k.wav

# Full 42 kHz core from a 96 kHz source
fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 2 -b 320000 --uncap-bandwidth --core-cutoff 40000 -o out.m4a in96k.wav

The last one reports core bandwidth: 42000 Hz in --verbose: the requested 40000 Hz is snapped up to the next scalefactor band edge.

Switch reference

This is the reference. Every switch gets its own card, and every card is built the same way, so you can read only the card you need.

How a card reads

The heading is the switch as you type it, with its argument. Under it:

  • A single bold sentence: what the switch is for, in terms of sound or behaviour.
  • Applies to: the profiles and situations where the switch has an effect. If a switch does nothing in a profile, it says so here.
  • Values: the legal range, the unit, and the default. The default is always the value that means "stock FDK decides".
  • Input: whether the switch takes a whole number, a decimal, a flag or text.
  • Then the explanation: the problem first, the mechanism in plain words, then what happens as you move the value up and down.
  • Examples: for a numeric switch with room to move, a mild, a strong and a deliberately exaggerated setting in each direction. Enumerated switches get a list of every value instead.
  • Works with / against: interactions, and which other switch masks this one.
  • Measured: only real measurements, with the material and conditions. The line is missing when there is no measurement.
  • Under the hood: one or two lines for the curious. Skip it freely.

Shared conventions

These hold for every card and are not repeated in each one.

Off = stock. Every Frankenstein switch is off unless you give it. With none of them on the command line, the output is byte identical to stock FDK. "Off" is spelled -1 for almost all of them (the sentinel meaning "leave FDK's own decision"), and 0 for --core-cutoff, --side-bias, --side-knee and --mask-slope, where -1 would be a legal working value.

Decimal input. Decimal switches accept . or ,, up to three decimal places. The value is rounded half away from zero, and the range is then checked on the rounded value. Anything else is an error: 1e1, nan, 3x, embedded spaces. Whole number switches reject fractions, so 128,5 where an integer is expected is an error, not a silent truncation.

Units. Four conventions appear throughout:

  • dB: a plain decibel offset, positive or negative.
  • x100: an integer that is a hundred times the real value, so 100 means 1.00, 90 means 0.90, 400 means 4.00.
  • Q8: a fixed point integer where 256 means 1.0. So 128 is 0.5, 384 is 1.5, 512 is 2.0, 4096 is 16.0. Q8 is FDK's own internal scale, which is why it leaks into the command line.
  • SFB: a scalefactor band number, counted from 0 at the bottom of the spectrum upwards. A scalefactor band is a group of spectral lines that share one scalefactor, roughly following critical bandwidth: narrow at the bottom, wide at the top. How many bands exist depends on profile, sample rate and the coded bandwidth, so an SFB number is only meaningful once you know that count. --verbose prints it, for example active SFBs (long): 49 at AAC-LC 48 kHz stereo.

Checking what took effect. --verbose prints the effective, initialised encoder parameters before encoding starts, plus a line franken overrides applied listing the switches that actually deviate from stock in this run. If a value you set does not show up there, it did not take effect, and no amount of listening will tell you that faster.

How -b is read

Three conventions live side by side, and mixing them up is the most common way to get a surprising file size.

  • Normal operation: -b is bits per second, but the frontend accepts the short form too. Small numbers are read as kilobits, so -b 128 and -b 128000 both mean 128 kbps, and -b 1152 means 1152 kbps.
  • With --unlock-bitrate: -b is read literally as bits per second, with no kilobit shortcut. -b 6000 is 6000 bps. -b 1152 in this mode is 1152 bps, which is practically nothing; the program warns when a value looks like that mistake.
  • With --dab: -b is kilobits per second, in the range 8..192 and a multiple of 8. The encoder multiplies by 1000 itself, so -b 96 is 96 kbps, also together with --unlock-bitrate.

The upper ceiling is not a switch and cannot be lifted: an AAC frame holds at most 6144 bits per channel. At 48 kHz that is roughly 576 kbps per channel.

Transport note for LD and ELD

AAC-LD (-p 23) and AAC-ELD (-p 39) work in this encoder, but they cannot be written into ADTS. Asking for -f 2 with those profiles ends in "encoder initialization failed", and the failure is the transport format, not the profile. Use M4A (-f 0, the default), LATM (-f 6 or -f 7) or LOAS (-f 10). LOAS is the practical choice when you want a bare stream to measure or to feed somewhere, because it is a self contained byte stream the way ADTS is for LC and HE-AAC.

Map of the groups

The switches are grouped in --help roughly from easy to geeky, and this reference follows the same order.

  • A. Start here: the consumer knobs. Diagnostics, intensity stereo in one slider, speech tuning, and the two opt-in flags that remove built-in limits.
  • B. Stereo coding: mid/side, intensity stereo in detail, parametric stereo.
  • C. Bandwidth and SBR: the high band, its detectors, estimators and grid.
  • D. Masking, noise shaping and detail: the psychoacoustic thresholds, the SNR the encoder demands per band, TNS and PNS.
  • E. Core transients, pre-echo and spectral holes: the LC, LD and ELD core under bit pressure.
  • F. Block switching and bitrate control: the long/short decision, the bit reservoir and the quasi-VBR knobs.
  • G. DAB+ output: digital radio superframes.

A. Start here

Five switches. One of them only looks at the encoder, two of them tune it, and two remove limits that stock FDK enforces. If you are new to this build, read --verbose first and use it after every other switch you try.

--verbose

Prints the encoder's real, initialised settings to stderr before encoding, so you can see what actually took effect.

  • Applies to: every profile and every transport. Changes nothing in the output stream.
  • Values: flag, no argument; default: off
  • Input: flag

The encoder makes a large number of decisions from your command line, and most of them are not the values you typed. Bandwidth gets snapped to a scalefactor band boundary. Bitrate selects a row in a tuning table that in turn sets PNS, intensity stereo and the SBR grid. A switch you spelled correctly may still be ignored because the profile has no such mechanism. --verbose is the only way to see the result of all that before you spend time listening.

What it prints: the profile (AOT), bitrate and bitrate mode, sample rate, channel mode, the effective core bandwidth in Hz together with its source (from -w, from --core-cutoff, or auto), afterburner state, transport and signaling, and the state of the coding tools the encoder picked for itself, that is TNS, PNS, intensity stereo and MS stereo on or off. When SBR is active it adds the SBR ratio, the effective start and stop frequency indices, the frequency scale, noise bands, amplitude resolution and, for the whole band, a final BW (AAC+SBR) estimate. It prints the reservoir budget block, which tells you the average bits per frame and the ceilings, and it prints active SFBs (long), which is the number you need before setting any SFB based switch. At the end comes the list franken overrides applied, naming only the switches that really deviate from stock in this run.

Three readings repay attention. The core bandwidth is SFB anchored, so a typed -w 17300 can come back as 17915 Hz: the encoder cannot cut inside a band. Under SBR the -w value is ignored but still labelled [from -w], which is confusing until you remember that --core-cutoff is the switch that works there. And active SFBs (long) is the ceiling for --msbands, --isbands and every <sfb> argument: asking for band 48 when only 40 are coded is not an error, it simply does nothing.

Values:

  • --verbose: print the dump. There is no off switch, since omitting it is off.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --verbose -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: nothing. It is the diagnostic for everything else in this reference, and the fastest answer to "did my switch do anything at all".

Measured: at AAC-LC 48 kHz stereo it reports active SFBs (long): 49. At HE-AAC v2 48 kHz the default core cutoff comes out at 8613 Hz.

--is-aggression <0..100>

One slider for how eagerly the encoder collapses upper bands to intensity stereo, trading stereo separation at the top for bits.

  • Applies to: profiles with a real stereo core, so AAC-LC, AAC-LD and AAC-ELD, and the core of HE-AAC. No effect on mono. Under HE-AAC v2 the stereo image is carried by parametric stereo on a mono core, so intensity stereo has nothing to act on.
  • Values: 0..100, where 0 is stock FDK; default: -1, off (identical to stock)
  • Input: whole number

Intensity stereo (IS) replaces separate left and right spectra in the upper bands with one shared energy envelope plus a direction. The ear localises high tones poorly, so this is cheap: you drop half the data in those bands and pay in stereo separation at the top. On material with genuinely different highs in the two channels, for instance cymbals placed hard to one side or wide spatial effects, you pay audibly.

Stock FDK is extremely shy about IS, which is why people conclude the switch does nothing. There are four gates, and a band needs to pass all of them at once: an admission gate that only considers IS when bitrate per band is below a threshold, a correlation threshold of about 0.95 between the channels, a minimum run of 6 consecutive bands, and a stable direction. Loosening one gate usually changes nothing, because another still blocks. --is-aggression moves all of them together, coherently: it drops the bitrate and bandwidth gate and loosens the correlation, direction and region requirements as the number rises.

Higher means more bands coded as IS, fewer bits spent on the top of the spectrum, and weaker stereo separation up there. 100 is the real maximum: gate removed, correlation loosened to roughly 0.475, a region of a single band is enough, and the direction tolerance is wide. Low values are not useful as a fine trim, because a small loosening often still leaves another gate binding; 1 changed nothing on our test material. The useful working window starts around 30.

There is only one direction to travel, since 0 is already stock and stock is close to "never".

Examples:

  • Mild, more IS: --is-aggression 30. Bands start going to IS on correlated material. Image at the top narrows slightly, a few percent of bits freed for the rest.
  • Strong, more IS: --is-aggression 50. Clearly more bands in IS. Audible narrowing on cymbals and reverb tails, a real bit saving at 48..96 kbps.
  • Exaggerated, more IS: --is-aggression 100. Almost anything that can go to IS does. The top of the image glues to the centre and panned highs drift. Useful at 32 kbps and below, and as a caricature to hear what IS sounds like when you let it run.

Works with / against: the explicit --is-* switches in group B override whatever the slider set, so use the slider first and only reach for --is-corr-thresh, --is-min-sfbs and --is-lr-ratio if you need precision. --is 0 disables intensity stereo outright and makes this switch irrelevant. --is-force-lo and --is-force-hi bypass every gate, so they do not need this slider at all. --verbose prints the effective correlation threshold and minimum band count, which is how you confirm the slider landed.

Measured: on mix48 at AAC-LC 64 kbps, 1 produced no stream change, while 30, 50 and 100 each changed the stream.

--speech

Switches the SBR layer to its speech tuning: different inverse filtering, a different noise floor, and no parametric coding of the high band.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC and HE-AAC v2, that is wherever SBR is running, including ELD with SBR. No effect on AAC-LC, which has no separate speech mode anywhere in FDK.
  • Values: flag; default: off (music tuning)
  • Input: flag

SBR rebuilds the high band from the coded low band, and it needs to know how tonal the source is to decide how much to whiten the patched band and how much noise to fill in. Music and speech have very different answers. Speech at low bitrate suffers a specific artifact when the music tuning is used: the reconstructed top gurgles, because the estimator keeps deciding the patch is tonal when it is really a moving formant plus breath.

The flag changes several things at once: the inverse filtering decision table switches to the speech table, the noise floor weighting goes to the speech value, and parametric coding of the high band is disabled. For a podcast, an audiobook or dialogue at 24..48 kbps the result is a more natural top with less gurgling.

If you want only part of what the flag does, the pieces are available separately: --sbr-invf-table 1 gives just the speech decision table, --sbr-nf-weight 1.0 just the speech noise weight, and --sbr-softness 4 both of them, in each case without touching the crossover and without disabling parametric coding.

For speech in plain AAC-LC there is no equivalent, and this reference will not pretend otherwise. The LC psychoacoustic model is the same for speech and music. What helps there instead is narrowing the band, since speech has little content high up, with --core-cutoff 12000 to 14000, and at very low bitrate turning on noise substitution with --pns 1 --force-pns.

Values:

  • omitted: music tuning (stock).
  • --speech: speech tuning.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 5 -b 32000 --speech -o speech.m4a podcast.wav

Works with / against: the explicit SBR estimator switches in group C win over it, so --sbr-invf forces a fixed inverse filtering level regardless. --sbr-invf-table and --sbr-nf-weight follow --speech when left at -1, which is how a single flag can move both.

Measured: no effect at all on AAC-LC; changes the HE-AAC stream.

--uncap-bandwidth

Removes FDK's hard 20 kHz limit on core bandwidth so --core-cutoff can reach up to Nyquist.

  • Applies to: any profile with a core you can widen, in practice AAC-LC at high bitrate from a high sample rate source. Meaningless without --core-cutoff.
  • Values: flag; default: off (the 20 kHz cap applies)
  • Input: flag

FDK clamps core bandwidth to min(20000, sr/2). The reasoning is sound for normal material: almost nobody hears above 20 kHz, and the bits are better spent lower. The consequence is that a 96 kHz source encoded at a generous bitrate still codes nothing above 20 kHz, no matter what you ask for, and the extra sample rate buys you only a gentler antialiasing slope.

This flag lifts the clamp. On its own it does nothing at all: it only raises the ceiling, it does not move the bandwidth. The switch that moves the bandwidth is --core-cutoff <hz>, and with the cap lifted that switch can go up to half the sample rate.

Be clear about what you are buying. Content above 20 kHz costs bits that come out of the audible band unless your bitrate has real headroom, and streams with bandwidth beyond the usual range sit outside typical practice, so some decoders may balk. This is a deliberate opt-in, which is why it is a separate flag rather than a side effect of --core-cutoff.

Values:

  • omitted: core bandwidth clamped at 20 kHz (stock).
  • --uncap-bandwidth: clamp lifted, --core-cutoff free up to sr/2.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 2 -b 320000 --uncap-bandwidth --core-cutoff 40000 --ath-scale 200 \
    -o out.m4a in96k.wav

Works with / against: requires --core-cutoff. Under SBR, -w is ignored and --core-cutoff is the switch that works, so the pair is also the way to widen a core sitting under SBR. Pairs naturally with --ath-scale below 256 when you have bitrate to spare, since there is no point coding 40 kHz of band at thresholds tuned for scarcity.

Measured: 96 kHz input, AAC-LC 400 kbps, broadband noise. Without the flag, --core-cutoff 40000 gives 20000 Hz in --verbose and essentially zero energy above 20 kHz. With the flag, --verbose reports 40000 Hz and the decoded file carries about 10 percent of its energy in 20..24 kHz, about 20 percent in 24..32 kHz and about 20 percent in 32..44 kHz. This encoder has no 96 kHz test signal checked in, so treat that as a prior measurement rather than one you can reproduce from the shipped material.

--unlock-bitrate

Removes the lower bitrate floors that every profile enforces, and makes -b literal bits per second.

  • Applies to: every profile. The floors removed differ by profile, see below.
  • Values: flag; default: off (all floors enforced)
  • Input: flag

Stock FDK refuses to encode below a floor, and the floor is different in each profile and is often mistaken for a format limit. It is not. The common claim that HE-AAC cannot go below 16 kbps describes the lowest row of the SBR tuning table for 48 kHz stereo, nothing more. Ask for less and stock FDK quietly raises your bitrate to that row.

The flag removes five separate floors:

  • the 40 bit per channel core minimum;
  • the LD and ELD rule of 8 kbps per channel, and the 208 bit transport reserve those profiles guess at (the real header overhead is still checked against the real transport encoder);
  • the start of the LD and ELD bandwidth table, so those profiles can use a band narrower than the table's first row;
  • the SBR and PS tuning table floor: HE-AAC, HE-AAC v2 and ELD with SBR below the table reuse its lowest row, keeping the same SBR grid while your bitrate is honoured and the AAC core simply gets fewer bits;
  • the rule that a frame unable to fit its own side information aborts the encode. Such frames overshoot instead, and the reservoir absorbs it over the following frames.

What remains is the transport header, 56 bits per frame for ADTS, and the upper ceiling of 6144 bits per channel, which no switch touches. Below the SBR payload estimate, about 2.5 kbps per channel plus parametric stereo, the core would have no bits at all and the encoder stops with a clean "encoder initialization failed" rather than an assertion.

The flag removes floors. It does not guarantee any particular bitrate. At these values dense passages routinely come out above what you asked for, because headers and side information alone cost more than the budget, so judge the result by the file size and not by the number in --verbose.

Mind the -b change. In unlock mode the number is bits per second, literally. -b 6000 is 6000 bps. -b 6 is 6 bps and will not encode anything useful, and -b 1152 typed in the belief that it means 1152 kbps gives you 1152 bps. Always write the full number of bits here.

Values:

  • omitted: all floors enforced, -b read with the kilobit shortcut (stock).
  • --unlock-bitrate: floors removed, -b literal bps.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 29 -b 6000 --unlock-bitrate -f 2 -o out.aac in48k.wav

Works with / against: --vbr-reservoir and --peak-bitrate become more interesting here, since overshoot is the normal case in this mode and the reservoir is what pays for it. With --dab, the DAB+ kilobit convention for -b is replaced by literal bps. Nothing in group D or F restores the floors.

Measured: real bitrate computed from the size of the ADTS or LOAS stream, with no container overhead:

Mode -b real bps FDK decoder ffmpeg 6.1
HE-AAC 48 kHz stereo 8000 / 6000 9232 / 7290 0 errors 0 errors
HE-AAC 48 kHz mono 4000 / 3000 4736 / 4348 0 0
HE-AAC v2 48 kHz 6000 / 4000 6678 / 4894 0 1 (see below)
ELD+SBR 48 kHz stereo 12000 12984 0 see below
ELD / LD 32 kHz stereo 8000 8154 0 see below
AAC-LC 48 kHz stereo 4000 / 3000 6012 / 7054 0 0

Where the real bitrate exceeds -b, those are dense frames overshooting on headers and side information alone. The ffmpeg 6.1 column needs reading with care: it reports the same errors on the stock encoder in these modes, one noise_facs_q is invalid on HE-AAC v2 at 12..14 kbps, which is inside the stock tables, and many errors on every ELD or LD LOAS stream at any bitrate. ffmpeg does not implement LD and ELD fully, so judge those streams with the Fraunhofer decoder.

B. Stereo coding

Three different machines in this encoder turn two channels into fewer bits, and they work on different parts of the signal, with different costs. Before reaching for any switch in this group, get clear on which of the three you are actually talking about.

MS (mid/side) is a lossless rotation of the channel pair. Instead of left and right, the encoder stores mid (L+R) and side (L-R) for a given scalefactor band. Nothing is thrown away: the decoder rotates back and gets L/R exactly. On correlated music the side signal is small, so it quantises to far fewer bits than a second full channel would. MS is a decision about bit economy, not about sound, and it is taken per band and per frame. Available in the AAC core, which means LC, LD, ELD, and the core under HE-AAC when that core is still stereo.

IS (intensity stereo) is lossy and directional. Over a range of upper bands the encoder stops sending two spectra and sends one energy envelope plus a direction, so the decoder paints both channels from the same spectrum with a pan coefficient. That is cheap and the ear tolerates it up high, where it localises fine spectral detail poorly. It also genuinely removes information: anything that differed between left and right in those bands, cymbals placed hard to one side, spatial effects, out-of-phase content, collapses toward a single steered source. IS lives in the core too, and the encoder is cautious with it by default.

PS (parametric stereo) is the HE-AAC v2 machine. The core becomes mono: one AAC spectrum, one SBR high band, and the stereo image rides along as a handful of parameters per frame (level differences, coherence, optionally phase). Below roughly 32 kbps that buys you a usable stereo picture where real two-channel coding would be starving. PS is not a refinement of MS or IS, it replaces them: with -p 29 there is no side channel to bias and no intensity bands to place, so every --ms* and --is* switch in this group is inert. Conversely --ps* does nothing outside HE-AAC v2.

In practice: at 96 kbps and up MS is doing almost all the work and you tune the mid/side bit split (--side-bias, --side-knee). Between about 40 and 80 kbps intensity stereo starts to earn its keep in the top bands (--is-aggression, then the --is* placement switches). Below about 32 kbps stereo, HE-AAC v2 and the PS parameters are the sane route, and the interesting knobs there are the resolution ones (--ps-env-reduce, --ps-bands).

Reading SFB numbers. Every band switch here counts scalefactor bands from 0 at the BOTTOM of the spectrum. Band 0 is bass, the highest number is the top of the coded range. How many bands exist depends on profile and sample rate: at LC 48 kHz stereo --verbose prints active SFBs (long): 49, so legal band numbers run 0..48. Always read that line before setting a range, because the count changes with the configuration and a number you remembered from another encode may be out of the spectrum entirely.

Short blocks. The numbers you give are always LONG-block bands. A short frame has its own, much coarser table (around 14 bands at 44.1 and 48 kHz, counted from 0 inside each window group), so the same number would mean another frequency there. The encoder therefore moves every range to the short table by frequency: each edge goes to the nearest short band edge in Hz, an exact tie goes up. A short band is in the range when at least half of it is. A narrow range that falls inside one wide short band takes that band. The result is the same region of the spectrum in long and short frames, for every sample rate, for DAB+ (960/120) and for every --short-groups setting. --verbose prints both, for example long 18..30 (2531..9000 Hz) -> short 3..7 (2250..8250 Hz). Up to 1.4.0-beta.1 the numbers were compared directly: --msbands-lo 18 left short frames with no MS at all, and --msbands 18 (MS up to 2.5 kHz) gave short frames MS up to 24 kHz.

Why a range above the coded bandwidth does nothing. The SFB count is the full table for the profile, but only bands up to the coded bandwidth carry anything. Bands above the cutoff are zero, and a stereo decision about a band of zeros changes no bits at all. This is a real trap with the high-band examples: at the default LC bandwidth of about 15.75 kHz, --msbands-lo 44 --msbands-hi 48 produces exactly the same stream as --msmask 0, because bands 44..48 sit above the coded band and MS is therefore switched off everywhere that matters. Force the band open with -w 20000 and the two stop being equal. So check your range against the coded bandwidth, not just against the SFB count.

All switches in this group are off unless you give them. Off means stock: the sentinel -1, and 0 for --side-bias, --side-knee and --mask-slope, leaves FDK's own decision alone. --side-bias, --side-knee and --mask-slope take decimals, with . or , and up to 3 decimal places; the rest are whole numbers and reject fractions.

MS

MS costs nothing in sound quality, so there is no "MS damages the image" tradeoff to manage. What you can manage is where MS is allowed and how the bit budget is split between the mid and the side channel once a band is in MS. Two of these switches are the real control (--msmask, and the band range), two are honest curiosities (--ms-bias, --ms-precision), and three are the quality knobs that repay study (--side-bias, --side-knee, --mask-slope).

--msmask <n>

Force the mid/side decision globally instead of letting the encoder choose per band.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core (LC, LD, ELD, and the stereo core under HE-AAC). No effect in HE-AAC v2, where the core is mono.
  • Values: -1 auto (default, FDK decides per band), 0 off (pure L/R everywhere), 1 on (MS in every band); default -1
  • Input: whole number

The encoder normally tests each band both ways and keeps whichever packs smaller. That is close to optimal for file size, and because the transform is lossless it is also close to irrelevant for sound. Two situations make the forced settings worth having anyway.

--msmask 0 guarantees that the coder never mixes your channels. Quantisation noise then stays inside the channel that caused it, instead of being shared between mid and side and redistributed to both outputs on reconstruction. That matters if the file will later be put through center cancellation, vocal removal, or any other subtraction of one channel from the other: with pure L/R the residual is your material plus its own noise, with MS it is your material plus noise that was fitted to the sum and difference. It costs bits, and on correlated music it costs real bits.

--msmask 1 is the opposite extreme, MS forced even where the encoder judged L/R cheaper. Useful as a reference point and for strongly correlated material, slightly wasteful on material with genuinely independent channels, for instance two unrelated mono sources hard panned.

Values:

  • -1: stock. Per band decision from the encoder's own cost comparison.
  • 0: pure L/R in every band. Nothing is mixed between channels.
  • 1: MS in every band.
fdkaac -p 2 -b 128000 --msmask 0 in.wav -o lr.m4a

Works with / against: --msmask 0 makes the whole mid/side family inert, including --msbands, --side-bias, --side-knee and --mid-bias, because no band is in MS. --msbands/--msbands-lo/--msbands-hi narrow where the chosen mask may apply.

Measured: on a 2 second sample the difference between --msmask 0 and --msmask 1 is about 900 bytes of payload, which puts the whole MS decision in perspective next to --ms-bias at about 2 bytes.

--msbands <n>

Allow MS only in the N lowest scalefactor bands and leave everything above in L/R.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 no limit (default), or 0..N where N is the active SFB count; n means bands 0..n-1 may use MS
  • Input: whole number

This is the "from the bottom up to" form of the band limit. It is counted from the bass upward, always, so --msbands 6 means bands 0 to 5 may be coded mid/side and bands 6 and above stay strictly L/R. --msbands 0 allows MS nowhere, which is the same thing as --msmask 0.

Why restrict MS at all, given that it is lossless? Because the bit split follows the mask. Inside MS bands the encoder allocates against the mid and side thresholds, and the side channel of a band is only as good as the bits the budget allows it. Keeping the top of the spectrum in L/R keeps the channel separation up there independent of that negotiation, which is a defensible choice on material whose high content really is uncorrelated. It also pairs well with high band experiments: L/R up top and MS down low is a different balance of risks from the encoder's own per band choice.

The difference from --msbands-lo/--msbands-hi is only the shape of the region: this switch always starts at band 0, the pair can start anywhere.

Examples:

  • Mild restriction: --msbands 40. Only the very top bands are pushed back to L/R; on most material the stream barely moves, because those bands often carried little.
  • Strong restriction: --msbands 12. MS confined to roughly the bass and low mids. Expect a measurably larger file at the same quality target, or coarser quantisation at the same bitrate.
  • Exaggerated restriction: --msbands 2. MS on essentially nothing. A caricature: you are paying nearly the full price of independent stereo while still signalling MS in two bands.

Works with / against: equal to --msbands-lo 0 --msbands-hi n-1 (--msbands 6 and --msbands-lo 0 --msbands-hi 5 give the same stream). Inert under --msmask 0. A limit above the active SFB count is the same as no limit.

--msbands-lo <n>

Lowest scalefactor band of the range in which MS is allowed.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 off (default), or 0..N within the active SFB count; pair it with --msbands-hi
  • Input: whole number

Together with --msbands-hi this defines a from..to window: MS may be used in bands lo..hi inclusive and nowhere else. Unlike --msbands, the window does not have to touch the bottom of the spectrum, which is the only way to put MS exclusively high up, for instance to let the encoder merge correlated noise and air at the top while the bass and mids stay in fully independent L/R.

Give both halves of the pair. --msbands-lo on its own leaves the upper edge unset, which is not a range.

Examples:

  • Mild, window starting low: --msbands-lo 2 --msbands-hi 48. Almost everything may use MS, the two lowest bands are excluded. Near stock in size.
  • Strong, window in the middle: --msbands-lo 10 --msbands-hi 30. MS only across the mids. Bass and top are L/R, so the file grows and the top keeps full separation.
  • Exaggerated, window at the very top: --msbands-lo 44 --msbands-hi 48 with -w 20000. MS on five bands of air only. A caricature of the band range feature, and the clearest demonstration of why the coded bandwidth matters: without -w 20000 these bands are above the default 15.75 kHz cutoff and the setting degenerates into --msmask 0.

Works with / against: must be read against --verbose active SFBs and against the coded bandwidth. Inert under --msmask 0. Overlaps --msbands, which is the same feature anchored at band 0.

--msbands-hi <n>

Highest scalefactor band, inclusive, of the range in which MS is allowed.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 off (default), or 0..N within the active SFB count; pair it with --msbands-lo
  • Input: whole number

The upper edge of the from..to window described above, and inclusive: --msbands-hi 5 includes band 5. Bands above it go to plain L/R.

In short blocks the range covers the same frequencies on the short table (see Short blocks above), so --msbands-lo 18 --msbands-hi 30 at 48 kHz is short bands 3..7.

Examples:

  • Mild: --msbands-lo 0 --msbands-hi 44. Top four bands excluded from MS. Small change.
  • Strong: --msbands-lo 0 --msbands-hi 20. MS up to roughly the presence region, L/R above.
  • Exaggerated: --msbands-lo 0 --msbands-hi 0. MS in exactly one band, the bass. Functionally independent stereo with one band of bookkeeping.

Works with / against: --msbands-lo is the other half; without it there is no range. A value above the active SFB count is the same as no upper limit. Inert under --msmask 0.

--ms-bias <n>

Nudge the L/R versus MS cost comparison toward MS.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 off (default, stock comparison), 0 also stock, 1..255 in Q8 where 256 would be 1.0 in FDK's internal log units, so 128 is +0.5
  • Input: whole number

Be honest with this one: it is a binary nudge dressed as a slider, and it is the weakest switch in the group. Measurement says so plainly. 0 gives the stock stream; every value from 1 to 255 gives one and the same different stream. There is no gradient to ride, so there is nothing to tune.

The reason is structural. MS is lossless, so this threshold does not decide how a band sounds, only which of two encodings is cheaper to write. The encoder's own comparison is already close to optimal, so a shift of the decision line can only flip bands that were nearly tied, and nearly tied bands cost nearly the same either way. The net effect on file size is a fraction of a percent, and the channel correlation after decoding moves in the fourth decimal place.

Use it to confirm that the decision point exists, not to shape sound. For real control over MS, --msmask 0 or --msmask 1 and the band range switches do the job properly, and --side-bias is the knob that actually changes what you hear.

Values:

  • -1 or 0: stock cost comparison.
  • 1..255: one single alternative stream, slightly more eager to pick MS.
fdkaac -p 2 -b 128000 --ms-bias 128 in.wav -o biased.m4a

Works with / against: pointless together with --msmask 0 or --msmask 1, which remove the decision entirely. Superseded for any practical purpose by --msmask and --side-bias.

Measured: mix48 at 128 kbps and tonal48/sparse48 at 96 kbps, values 1..255 all produce an identical stream that differs from 0 only. Size effect under 0.1 percent, on the order of 2 bytes on a 2 second sample.

--ms-precision <n>

Scale the quantisation precision of MS bands as a whole, mid and side together.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core, MS bands only. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: Q8 multiplier from 256 (no change) upward, 384 is about 1.5x, 512 about 2x, no upper limit; values below 256 are rejected; -1 off (default)
  • Input: whole number

This is the closest thing here to a LAME style global quality lever for the stereo pair: raise it and the thresholds in MS bands get tighter, which means shallower spectral holes and finer quantisation in both mid and side at once. It is blunt, because it moves the mid and the side in the same direction and by the same factor, so it cannot shift the balance between them, which is usually what you actually want.

Its reach is also limited in two ways worth knowing before you spend time on it. Above roughly 600 to 800 the thresholds run into FDK's own hard floor and further increases stop doing anything. And under CBR the bit budget does not grow, so a tighter threshold in MS bands mostly moves bits from somewhere else in the same frame rather than adding detail. The effect flattens out into a reshuffle.

Reach for --side-bias instead when the goal is stereo quality: it acts per channel, at the decision that actually drops or keeps a side band.

Examples:

  • Mild: --ms-precision 320. Slightly tighter MS thresholds, roughly 1.25x. Small reduction in zeroed bands inside MS.
  • Strong: --ms-precision 512. About 2x. Noticeably fewer holes in MS bands; under CBR expect the gain to be paid for elsewhere in the frame.
  • Exaggerated: --ms-precision 2048. Deep into the saturated region, where the threshold floor has already taken over. A caricature: the number looks dramatic and the sound stops tracking it. There is no other direction: the switch only tightens. To make MS bands coarser on purpose, raise the side threshold with a negative --side-bias, or the mid threshold with --mid-bias.

Works with / against: inert under --msmask 0. Overlaps --mid-bias and --side-bias, which move one channel at a time; if you are using those, leave this at default.

Under the hood: a Q8 multiplier applied to the MS band thresholds, hence 256 meaning 1.00.

--mid-bias <n>

Deliberately raise the mid channel threshold so the freed bits can go to the side.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core, MS bands. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: Q8, 256 = off, above 256 raises the mid threshold, no upper limit; -1 off (default)
  • Input: whole number

After the MS rotation the mid channel carries the sum, which on normal music is most of the loudness and most of the information people listen to. This switch raises its masking threshold on purpose, which makes the mid cheaper to code and leaves more of a fixed budget for the side. It is a legitimate way to buy stereo width, and a risky one, because you are paying with the channel that carries the melody.

Use it sparingly, and prefer --side-bias for the same job. Both pull on the same mid/side budget, but --side-bias pulls from the side end, where the measurement is cleaner and where the effect of overdoing it shows up as a narrower image rather than a duller lead vocal.

Examples:

  • Mild: --mid-bias 288. Mid threshold raised slightly, a little headroom handed to the side. Hard to fault on dense material.
  • Strong: --mid-bias 384. About 1.5x. The side gets a real share; the mid starts to lose definition at low bitrate.
  • Exaggerated: --mid-bias 1024. The mid is given away to feed the difference signal. A caricature: a wide, hollow image with a soft centre, which is almost never what anyone wants from a music encode.

Works with / against: inert under --msmask 0, and pointless in combination with a large positive --side-bias, since both spend the same budget from opposite ends. Overlaps --ms-precision, which moves mid and side together instead.

--side-bias <dB>

Shift the side channel masking threshold on MS bands: the main stereo width versus centre quality lever.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core, MS bands. No effect in HE-AAC v2, and nothing to do under --msmask 0.
  • Values: -24.0..50.0 dB, 0 = off (default, stock threshold)
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

This is the switch to learn in this group. The encoder decides, per scalefactor band, whether the side signal is worth coding by comparing its energy against a masking threshold; below the threshold the band is dropped to zero. That one comparison is where your stereo width lives. Moving the threshold moves the line.

A positive value lowers the effective bar for the side channel: fewer side bands are zeroed, the survivors are quantised more finely, and what you hear is cleaner width, reverb tails that decay instead of stopping, and ambience that stays put instead of collapsing to the centre. It is not free. The bit budget is fixed, so those bits come out of the mid channel, and at low bitrate you can hear the centre give them up. That is the tradeoff working as designed, not a fault. The measurement below shows exactly this shape: the side error improves steadily from +1 to +9 while the mid error degrades steadily over the same span.

A negative value does the reverse and raises the bar, so side bands fall out and the image narrows toward mono. That is a deliberate tool for very low bitrate work, or for an effect, and it has one hard structural feature you need to know about: it does not narrow smoothly all the way down.

The step at about -0.97 dB. From 0 to -0.95 dB the side error moves smoothly, which is the gentle narrowing you would expect. At about -0.97 dB the raised side threshold crosses a minimum SNR line that the encoder keeps inside its hole avoidance logic, and most side bands drop out at once. Past that point the knob is finished: -1, -4 and -12 all give the same result, because the side has already been cut. This is a property of the encoder, a real discontinuity in its decision chain, not a rounding artefact of the command line: the decimals are read to 3 places and the value you type is the value used. The practical rule follows directly. For gentle narrowing stay inside 0 to -0.95, and treat anything at or beyond -0.97 as a single setting that means "drop the side".

In the positive direction the useful span ends around +9. Beyond that, measurement shows both channels getting worse: the side stops improving and the mid keeps degrading, which is a bad trade in both directions at once.

Examples:

  • Mild, more bits to the side: --side-bias 1. A touch more side detail, mid essentially untouched. Safe on anything.
  • Strong, more bits to the side: --side-bias 6. Clearly wider and more spacious, with the mid starting to pay for it at low bitrate. This is the normal working value.
  • Exaggerated, more bits to the side: --side-bias 24. Past the useful span. The side gains nothing further and the mid is measurably damaged, so the image gets wide and the centre gets thin. A caricature that shows where the budget went.
  • Mild, narrowing: --side-bias -0.3. Slightly tighter image, a few marginal side bands given up. The fine control lives in this region.
  • Strong, narrowing: --side-bias -0.9. Noticeably narrower, still inside the smooth region and still just short of the step.
  • Exaggerated, narrowing: --side-bias -4. Past the -0.97 dB step, so the side is simply gone and the result is near mono. A caricature, and identical to -1 or -12: there is nothing left to remove.

Works with / against: --side-knee shapes the edge of the same decision and combines cleanly with this. --mid-bias pushes on the same budget from the mid end, so using both hard is double counting. --msmask 0 removes the side channel entirely and makes this switch inert. --msbands/--msbands-lo/--msbands-hi limit which bands it can touch.

Measured: dense48, LC stereo 40 kbps, Fraunhofer decoder, error energy relative to the source per channel. Default band (9.75 kHz): side error -0.53, -0.58, -0.62, -0.63, -0.66 dB at 0, +1, +3, +6, +9, while mid error goes -8.62, -8.53, -8.27, -7.08, -6.43 dB. At +24 side gets worse again (-0.42 dB) and mid keeps losing (-5.81 dB). Negative direction with the band forced to 18 kHz (-w 18000): side error -1.13 dB at 0, -1.03 at -0.5, -0.93 at -0.9, -0.92 at -0.95, then -0.31 at -0.97 and the same at -1, -4 and -12. Which quantity shows the step depends on material and bandwidth: at the default band on the same signal the step shows in the mid error instead (-8.72 to -9.04 dB at -0.97).

--side-cutoff <Hz>

From this frequency up, code every stereo band as pure mid.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core (LC, LD, ELD). Off by default.
  • Values: 1000..24000 Hz, rounded to the scalefactor band that starts at or above it

Above the cutoff the side signal is dropped and the band is written as mid only, with the cheap MS mask. Pure L/R never happens there.

The level stays: both channels then play the mid, which alone carries less energy than the original channels whenever L and R differ (uncorrelated L/R would come out 3 dB quieter). So the mid is raised to the energy of the original, by at most 6 dB, and only where the side is above its masking threshold (a side that stock would zero anyway is not added). Measured at 96 kbps: a band 3 dB quieter than the source is 3 dB quieter with the cutoff too, for uncorrelated, partly correlated and correlated L/R. Up to 1.4.0-beta.1 the band lost up to 3 dB more. Two things mid cannot carry, whatever its level: content in anti-phase (L = -R, its mid is zero) and the direction of a hard panned sound, which ends up in the centre.

Intensity stereo above the cutoff: FDK's stock IS decision is cleared there, because pure mid is cheaper. IS stays when it is asked for explicitly:

  • --is-force-lo/-hi: IS on the given bands, always.
  • loosened IS criteria (--is-aggression, --is-corr-thresh, --is-lr-ratio, --is-min-sfbs, --is-admit above 5): FDK's own IS decision with those criteria. Single panned regions above the cutoff then keep their side (IS carries the direction and the phase per band), the rest stays mid, like the intensity stereo of MPEG-1 Layer II. Measured (40 kbps, --side-cutoff 1000, a 3..6 kHz source panned 4 dB left and right in turn over a mono mix): cutoff alone 0 dB, with --is-aggression 100 3..4 dB, as the source.

Bands that both channels code as PNS are left as they are. Below the cutoff stereo is untouched. --verbose prints the band where it starts, in the long and in the short table. Both round up to the next band edge, so on the coarse short table the edge can only move up (9 kHz: long band 31 at 9.0 kHz, short band 9 at 10.5 kHz), which destroys less side, never more.

Examples (acoustic stereo music):

  • Mild: -b 48000 --side-cutoff 8000. Side info 238 to 235 bits per frame, mid a little cleaner.
  • Strong: -b 48000 --side-cutoff 5000. Side info 222 bits, mid error 0.7 dB lower, side above 5 kHz about 27..31 dB down.
  • Exaggerated: -b 48000 --side-cutoff 1000. Practically mono with a stereo bass.

At 14 kbps the gain is small, because FDK already zeroes almost all of the side there; --is-admit 0 is the bigger lever at that rate.

--is-admit <x>

Move FDK's gate that decides whether intensity stereo may be used at all.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. Stock 5.
  • Values: 0.0..20.0; 0 = never. --is-aggression still opens the gate.

FDK allows intensity stereo only when bitrate divided by the core bandwidth (bits per second per Hz) is below 5. At very low rates IS is expensive: it forces the per-band MS mask (about 22 bits per frame) and adds IS positions. Measured at 14 kbps stereo on dense material: --is-admit 0 lowers zeroed bands from 85% to 70%.

--side-knee <dB>

Give every side band near the coded/zeroed edge a precision that follows its own level.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core, MS bands. No effect in HE-AAC v2, and nothing to do under --msmask 0.
  • Values: -24.0..50.0 dB, 0 = off (default, stock behaviour)
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

--side-bias moves where the coded/zeroed line sits. This switch works on the margin of each band: how far its energy lies above its masking threshold, which decides how finely the band is coded and whether it survives at all. The value is an offset added to that margin, so a band keeps the precision its real level deserves, just shifted.

One detail of FDK makes this necessary. When bits run out, FDK does not drop a weak band at once: it pins the band at about 0.97 dB above its threshold and keeps it at the coarsest step. On real music some 10% of the side bands end up stacked on that one value, whatever their real level was. The switch reads the margin the rate control wanted before that rescue, so those bands are spread out again by their real level instead of moving as one block. Measured on acoustic music at 96 kbps, coded side bands per frame fall evenly from 30.2 (stock) through 25.4 (-1), 22.2 (-3), 18.1 (-6), 10.5 (-12) to 1.1 (-24): no jump.

A positive value adds precision: bands up to N dB below the threshold are kept, graded by how far below they were, and bands near the edge get up to N dB finer. A negative value takes precision away: the bands closest to the edge leave first and the bits go to mid. The offset is full near the edge and fades out above it, so the loud, well coded part of the side channel is not touched (the fade-out zone is --side-knee-zone).

Examples (acoustic stereo music, 96 kbps):

  • Mild, finer: --side-knee 2. Side holes 6.9% to 2.2%, mid error up 0.8 dB.
  • Strong, finer: --side-knee 6. Side holes 0.6%, side error 2.4 dB lower, mid pays 3.5 dB.
  • Exaggerated, finer: --side-knee 24. More bits go to side than to mid. A caricature.
  • Mild, coarser: --side-knee -1. The weakest side bands go, about 5 of 37 per frame.
  • Strong, coarser: --side-knee -6. Side holes 29%, mid error 2.4 dB lower.
  • Exaggerated, coarser: --side-knee -24. Practically no side left; a mono image with stereo bookkeeping.

Diagnostic companions (may be removed in a later version):

  • --side-knee-zone <x>, 1.0..20.0, stock 3: the offset is full up to |knee| dB above the threshold and fades out linearly to zero at x times |knee| dB.
  • --side-knee-spread <dB>, 0.1..24.0, stock 1: on the negative side, how many dB of knee are needed to spread the rescued crowd fully by its real margin. Both changed little in measurement. Without --side-knee they do nothing.

Works with / against: orthogonal to --side-bias and designed to be used with it; bias sets where the threshold is, knee sets how soft its edge is. Inert under --msmask 0.

Measured: dense stereo LC. Negative values saturate at -6 dB (-6, -12 and -24 give an identical stream); positive values keep changing the stream across the range.

--mask-slope <dB>

Shift how far below the frame average a band must sit before the encoder stops caring about it.

  • Applies to: both channels, mid and side, in the AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -24.0..50.0 dB, 0 = off (default); distinct results only inside roughly -9.9..+9
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

FDK carries a non masking heuristic: when a scalefactor band's energy sits far below the average energy of the frame, more than about 10 dB below in stock tuning, the required SNR for that band is relaxed. In plain words, the encoder deliberately starves very quiet bands in order to spend the bits on the loud ones. Most of the time that is correct, because a band 20 dB under the rest of the frame is usually masked. Sometimes it is not, and what gets starved is the reverb tail, the room, or the decay of the previous note.

This switch shifts that "how far below average before I stop caring" line, for both channels at once. A positive value raises it, so fewer bands qualify as negligible and quiet detail survives. Quiet passages, decays and reverb tails keep more of their structure, and the bits come from somewhere else in the frame. A negative value lowers it, so more bands are starved and the result is leaner and hollower, with more bits for the loud material.

Set expectations honestly: this is a subtle control. It only touches the quietest bands in a frame, so on dense material the decoded error barely moves, and on our sparse probe only large values shifted it at all. It is most worth trying on sparse, reverberant or deliberately quiet material, and least worth trying on a loud dense mix.

The live range is narrower than the accepted range. Distinct results appear for roughly -9.9 to +9. At -10 and below the result stops changing, +10 to +40 collapse to one stream, and 45 to 50 to another. Keep your examples inside the live span, where the number actually corresponds to behaviour.

Examples:

  • Mild, protect quiet bands: --mask-slope 2. Slightly fewer bands starved. Expect tenths of a dB in measurement and a marginally more open decay.
  • Strong, protect quiet bands: --mask-slope 6. A real share of quiet bands kept. The most useful setting on reverberant material.
  • Exaggerated, protect quiet bands: --mask-slope 9. The top of the live range: almost nothing is treated as negligible, and bits are being spent on content that is very probably masked. A caricature, and the last value that still responds, since +10 and above all behave the same.
  • Mild, starve quiet bands: --mask-slope -2. A few more quiet bands given up. Slightly leaner.
  • Strong, starve quiet bands: --mask-slope -6. Quiet bands clearly starved, more bits for the loud content, a hollower sound on sparse material.
  • Exaggerated, starve quiet bands: --mask-slope -9.5. The bottom of the live range, quiet detail stripped wherever the heuristic can reach. A caricature of a lean setting, and the floor of the control, since -10 and below stop changing anything.

Works with / against: same family as --side-bias, but keyed on energy versus the frame average rather than on the side threshold, and applied to mid and side alike. Use one or the other while you learn what each does, then combine.

Measured: mix48 at 40 kbps gives distinct streams only for about -9.9..+9; -10 and below collapse to one stream, +10..+40 to another, 45..50 to a third. On sparse48 at 40 kbps the decoded error barely moves, with only +12 and +24 shifting it.

Intensity stereo (IS)

Intensity stereo is the lossy one. Across a run of upper bands the encoder stops sending two spectra and sends a single energy envelope plus a direction, and the decoder rebuilds both channels from that one spectrum. The saving is large, the cost is stereo separation in exactly those bands, and whether you can hear the cost depends entirely on what lives up there: a correlated cymbal wash survives it well, a hard panned effect or a wide reverb does not.

Stock FDK is very cautious with IS. There is not one gate but several, and a band is coded as intensity only when all of them pass at once: a bitrate per band admission rule, a correlation test between the channels, a minimum run of contiguous bands, and a stable direction. This is why moving a single threshold so often appears to do nothing, another gate is still blocking. Loosening --is-corr-thresh on its own, for instance, does not open IS.

Start with --is-aggression from group A. It is the consumer slider for exactly this problem: a single value from 0 to 100 that moves all of the gates together and coherently. 0 is stock, which in practice means IS barely activates at normal bitrates, and 100 is the real maximum: the bitrate gate dropped, the correlation requirement loosened, the minimum run reduced to a single band and the direction tolerance widened. Start around 30 to 50 and listen, then go up if the saving is worth more to you than the width. Only once you know what the slider does should you reach for the switches below, which override what it set.

--is <n>

Turn intensity stereo on or off globally instead of leaving it to the encoder.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core (LC, LD, ELD, and the stereo core under HE-AAC). No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 auto (default), 0 off, 1 on
  • Input: whole number

This is the master switch, and it is a permission, not a command: 1 allows intensity stereo, it does not place it. The gates described above still decide which bands actually go to IS, so --is 1 alone on material the encoder considers unsuitable, or at a bitrate above the admission rule, can leave the stream unchanged. Pair it with --is-aggression when you want IS to actually happen.

--is 0 is the useful end of this switch on its own. It guarantees no intensity coding at all, which is what you want when stereo separation in the top octaves matters more than the bits, or when you are isolating the effect of some other switch and do not want IS moving underneath the measurement.

Values:

  • -1: stock. The encoder decides, cautiously.
  • 0: intensity stereo never used. Full L/R or MS coding everywhere.
  • 1: intensity stereo allowed, subject to the usual gates.
fdkaac -p 2 -b 64000 --is 1 --is-aggression 40 in.wav -o is.m4a

Works with / against: --is-aggression is what makes --is 1 visible. --is-force-lo/ --is-force-hi bypass the gates entirely and work even without this switch. Completely independent stereo is --msmask 0 --is 0.

--isbands <n>

Cap how many scalefactor bands may be coded as intensity stereo.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 no limit (default), or a count within the active SFB count
  • Input: whole number

A ceiling on the total extent of intensity coding, expressed as a number of bands rather than as a position in the spectrum. It is the brake to use when IS is helping the bitrate but you want to bound the damage: let the encoder pick where intensity goes, but refuse to let it swallow half the spectrum.

Read the ceiling against --verbose active SFBs: at LC 48 kHz stereo there are 49 bands, so a cap of 40 is barely a cap at all and a cap of 4 is severe. Bands beyond the cap are coded normally.

In short frames the cap holds for every window group on its own (each group is a slice of time with its own copy of the band table) and is scaled to the coarser table: at 48 kHz --isbands 6 allows 2 short bands per group. Up to 1.4.0-beta.1 it counted across the groups, so the first groups of a transient frame used it up and the rest of the frame got no IS at all.

Examples:

  • Mild cap: --isbands 24. Half the spectrum may still go intensity. On most material this changes little, because IS rarely reaches that far.
  • Strong cap: --isbands 8. Intensity kept to a short run. Useful with a high --is-aggression, where it stops the slider from running away.
  • Exaggerated cap: --isbands 1. A single band of intensity. A caricature: you keep the signalling and lose essentially all of the saving.

Works with / against: --is-lo/--is-hi restrict where IS may go, this one restricts how much. They combine. Inert when IS never activates, so check with --is-aggression first.

--is-min-sfbs <n>

Minimum run of contiguous bands before intensity stereo is allowed to start.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 default, which is 6, or a count of bands
  • Input: whole number

One of the gates. The encoder refuses to start intensity coding on an isolated band or two; it wants a continuous region of at least this many bands that all pass the other tests. The reasoning is that a short intensity patch surrounded by normally coded bands is an audible seam, a small region where the image suddenly collapses and then reopens.

Lower the number and IS catches short fragments as well, which means it triggers far more often and on more material, with those seams as the risk. Raise it and IS becomes rarer: only wide, consistently correlated regions qualify, which is the conservative setting.

This is an advanced knob. --is-aggression already moves it as part of its coordinated sweep, so use this one only when you are tuning by hand with aggression at 0, and remember that an explicit value overrides what the slider set.

Examples:

  • Mild, more IS: --is-min-sfbs 4. Slightly shorter regions qualify. IS a little more frequent.
  • Strong, more IS: --is-min-sfbs 2. Short fragments accepted. Clearly more intensity coding, and the first point where seams become plausible.
  • Exaggerated, more IS: --is-min-sfbs 1. Any single band may become intensity. A caricature: intensity scattered through the spectrum one band at a time.
  • Mild, less IS: --is-min-sfbs 8. Slightly rarer than stock.
  • Strong, less IS: --is-min-sfbs 16. Only broad regions qualify; on most material IS almost never fires.
  • Exaggerated, less IS: --is-min-sfbs 40. A run that long essentially never exists, so this is intensity stereo switched off by the back door. A caricature of a conservative setting, and --is 0 says the same thing honestly.

Works with / against: one gate of several, so lowering it alone may change nothing if the correlation or bitrate gate still blocks. Overridden in effect by --is-force-lo/-hi, which bypass it. Prefer --is-aggression.

--is-corr-thresh <n>

How similar the two channels must be in a band before intensity stereo is allowed.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: Q8, where 256 = 1.0 correlation; -1 default, which is about 243, meaning roughly 0.95
  • Input: whole number

The correlation gate, and the one people reach for first. The stock requirement is severe: the two channels must be about 95 percent alike in a band before the encoder will consider replacing them with one envelope and a direction. Very little real music is that correlated in the top bands, which is most of the reason IS seems inactive by default.

The direction is counter intuitive, so read it twice: LOWER means MORE intensity. You are lowering the bar that the material has to clear, so more bands clear it. 180 is about 0.70, which is a lot more permissive than stock. Too low and you start coding genuinely different channels as one steered source, which is heard as direction errors and as the top of the image gluing together.

It is one gate among several, which has a practical consequence worth stating plainly: changing this on its own does not open intensity stereo. If the bitrate admission rule or the minimum run is still blocking, the stream will not move no matter what you set here.

Examples:

  • Mild, more IS: --is-corr-thresh 230. About 0.90. Slightly more permissive than stock.
  • Strong, more IS: --is-corr-thresh 180. About 0.70. Substantially more bands qualify; this is the usual hand tuning target.
  • Exaggerated, more IS: --is-corr-thresh 60. About 0.23, which accepts channels that are barely related. A caricature: expect audible direction errors wherever it fires.
  • Mild, less IS: --is-corr-thresh 250. Tighter than stock, IS slightly rarer.
  • Strong, less IS: --is-corr-thresh 254. Only nearly identical channels qualify.
  • Exaggerated, less IS: --is-corr-thresh 256. Perfect correlation demanded, which real material essentially never reaches. A caricature: intensity stereo disabled by an impossible test, where --is 0 would be clearer.

Works with / against: alone it does not open IS, since the other gates still apply. Moved automatically by --is-aggression, and an explicit value here overrides the slider. Bypassed by --is-force-lo/-hi.

--is-lr-ratio <n>

How even the left/right energy balance must be in a band before intensity stereo is allowed.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: Q8, where 256 = 1.0; -1 default, which is about 179, meaning roughly 0.7
  • Input: whole number

The panning gate. Correlation says the two channels have the same shape; this says they have comparable level. A band where one side carries almost everything is a badly chosen place for intensity coding, because the single shared spectrum plus a direction has to represent a strongly lopsided pair, and the direction parameter carries the whole burden.

Lower values accept more lopsided bands and therefore produce more intensity coding, in the same counter intuitive direction as the correlation threshold. Higher values demand a more even balance and make IS rarer.

This is the third of the three hand tuning thresholds and the one to set last. Work in the order correlation, minimum run, then ratio: the first two decide whether IS fires at all, this one mostly decides where.

Examples:

  • Mild, more IS: --is-lr-ratio 160. Slightly more tolerant of uneven bands.
  • Strong, more IS: --is-lr-ratio 110. Clearly lopsided bands accepted, more intensity placement across the spectrum.
  • Exaggerated, more IS: --is-lr-ratio 20. Almost any imbalance accepted. A caricature: hard panned content gets reduced to one spectrum plus a pan value, which is precisely where IS sounds worst.
  • Mild, less IS: --is-lr-ratio 200. A bit stricter than stock.
  • Strong, less IS: --is-lr-ratio 240. Only well balanced bands qualify; IS becomes rare on anything with a wide mix.
  • Exaggerated, less IS: --is-lr-ratio 256. Exact level equality demanded. A caricature that switches IS off through a test nothing passes.

Works with / against: one gate of several; alone it rarely changes the stream. Moved by --is-aggression, overridden by an explicit value. Bypassed by --is-force-lo/-hi.

--is-lo <sfb>

Allow intensity stereo only from this scalefactor band upward.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 off (default), or 0..N within the active SFB count
  • Input: whole number

A placement restriction, not a trigger. Bands below the given number stay L/R or MS no matter what the gates say; bands at or above it remain subject to the normal gates. This switch can only take intensity coding away, never add it.

The obvious use is protecting the bottom of the spectrum. Intensity stereo high up is a reasonable trade, intensity stereo in the mids or the bass is not, because that is where the ear localises by level and timing and where a collapsed image is immediately heard. There is a trap worth knowing: at low bitrate the encoder tends to place IS on relatively LOW bands, lower than intuition suggests, so if you want to see this switch do anything, scan small values rather than starting at 30.

Examples:

  • Mild, push IS upward: --is-lo 10. Bass and low mids protected. Often enough to remove the worst placements.
  • Strong, push IS upward: --is-lo 25. Intensity confined to the upper half of the spectrum. A sensible default for careful work at middling bitrate.
  • Exaggerated, push IS upward: --is-lo 46. Intensity permitted only in the top few bands, which at the default coded bandwidth may be above the coded range entirely. A caricature, and the same trap as the MS high band example: check it against the coded bandwidth or it silently means "no IS at all".

Works with / against: pairs with --is-hi as a window. Restricts only, so it cannot make IS happen; use --is-aggression for that. Has nothing to do with --is-force-lo, which is the forcing switch with a similar name.

--is-hi <sfb>

Allow intensity stereo only up to this scalefactor band, inclusive.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 off (default), or 0..N within the active SFB count
  • Input: whole number

The upper edge of the permitted intensity window, inclusive, and the same kind of restriction as --is-lo: it narrows where the encoder may place IS and never forces it. Together the pair defines a from..to region, everything outside stays conventionally coded.

Capping from above is the less common direction, but it has a clear use. If the very top of your spectrum carries the stereo information you care about most, air, cymbal placement, room, you can let intensity take the busy upper mids and keep the top bands in real stereo.

Examples:

  • Mild cap: --is-hi 44. Only the topmost bands excluded from intensity.
  • Strong cap: --is-hi 30. Intensity confined below the top of the spectrum; the air band keeps genuine separation.
  • Exaggerated cap: --is-hi 3. A window so low that the gates essentially never fire inside it. A caricature that amounts to disabling IS, where --is 0 is clearer and cheaper to reason about.

Works with / against: --is-lo is the other half of the window. Combines with --isbands, which limits the count rather than the position. Does not interact with the forced range.

--is-force-lo <sfb>

Force intensity stereo from this band upward, bypassing every gate.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 off (default), or 0..N within the active SFB count; pair with --is-force-hi
  • Input: whole number

This is the laboratory switch, and it behaves differently from everything above it. The correlation test, the minimum run, the level balance rule and the bitrate admission rule are all skipped: the bands in the forced range are coded as intensity because you said so. It works with no other IS switch set, so it does not need --is 1 and it ignores --is-aggression.

Understand what that means for the sound. Intensity coding keeps one spectrum and a direction, so within the forced range the channels can no longer differ in anything except level and sign. On uncorrelated content the result is not subtle; it is a deliberate, audible collapse of the image, and it can sound badly wrong. That is the point of the switch: it lets you hear and measure the mechanism at full strength, and it lets you build a specific effect. The stream stays standards compliant, so every decoder will play it.

Pair it with --is-force-hi. Set it high in the spectrum first to hear what intensity does where it is meant to be used, then walk it downward to hear why the gates exist.

Examples:

  • Mild force: --is-force-lo 40 --is-force-hi 48. Intensity on the top bands only, which is roughly where the encoder would put it anyway. Closest to a usable setting.
  • Strong force: --is-force-lo 20 --is-force-hi 48. The whole upper half forced to intensity. Clear narrowing of the image above the mids, and a real bitrate saving.
  • Exaggerated force: --is-force-lo 0 --is-force-hi 48. The entire spectrum coded as one steered channel. A caricature and a useful demonstration: this is what the stereo image costs you, all at once.

Works with / against: bypasses --is-corr-thresh, --is-min-sfbs, --is-lr-ratio and the bitrate gate, so none of them limit it. Distinct from --is-lo, which only restricts. Needs --is-force-hi to define the top of the range.

Measured: --is-force-lo and --is-force-hi change the stream with no other IS switch given.

--is-force-hi <sfb>

Upper band, inclusive, of the forced intensity range.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC core. No effect in HE-AAC v2.
  • Values: -1 off (default), or 0..N within the active SFB count; pair with --is-force-lo
  • Input: whole number

The other half of the forced range, inclusive of the band you name. Both halves together define the region that is coded as intensity regardless of what the material looks like.

Keep the range inside the coded bandwidth. Forcing intensity on bands above the cutoff forces it on silence, which changes nothing and will have you chasing a switch that is working perfectly.

Examples:

  • Mild: --is-force-lo 44 --is-force-hi 46. Three bands of forced intensity near the top. A narrow probe for A/B listening.
  • Strong: --is-force-lo 30 --is-force-hi 48. A wide forced region through the upper spectrum.
  • Exaggerated: --is-force-lo 5 --is-force-hi 48. Everything above the bass forced. A caricature: the bass keeps its stereo and everything else is steered mono, which sounds exactly as odd as it reads.

Works with / against: meaningless without --is-force-lo. Independent of the gate switches and of --is-lo/--is-hi. Check the range against --verbose active SFBs and against the coded bandwidth.

Parametric stereo (PS)

Parametric stereo is what HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) uses, and it is a different proposition from everything above. The waveform coder sees one channel: the two inputs are downmixed to mono, the mono signal gets an AAC core plus SBR, and the stereo image travels separately as a small set of parameters per frame. The decoder takes the mono signal, decorrelates it, and rebuilds a left and a right from those parameters.

The parameters are few and specific. IID is the level difference between the channels per parameter band, which is what places a level panned source. ICC is the coherence, how alike the two channels are, which is what decides whether a band sounds like a point source or like a wide diffuse field. Optionally IPD carries the phase difference between the channels, and OPD says where that rotation sits relative to the downmix. That is the whole stereo picture: a few dozen numbers per frame rather than a second spectrum.

This is why PS works at bitrates where nothing else does. At 24 or 32 kbps a real stereo core would be starving both channels; a mono core at the same rate is comfortable, and the parameters cost almost nothing. It is also why PS has its own failure modes, which have nothing to do with quantisation noise: the image can be too coarsely sampled in frequency (too few bands), too coarsely sampled in time (too few envelopes), or momentarily absent (frames sent with no parameters at all). The switches below address exactly those three.

If you take one setting from this section, take --ps-env 4 --ps-env-reduce 0. Asking for four envelopes alone often changes nothing, because an automatic reduction loop collapses them again; switching that loop off is what delivers the improvement.

Everything here needs -p 29. Under LC, HE-AAC v1, LD or ELD there is no PS layer and these switches do nothing.

--ps <n>

Force the IID stereo parameter to be sent, or forbid it, instead of leaving it to the encoder's heuristic.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: -1 auto (default), 0 off, 1 on
  • Input: whole number

IID is the level difference parameter, the backbone of the PS image. The encoder decides whether to send it from a loudness difference heuristic: if the two channels sit at nearly the same level it may conclude there is nothing to describe and send nothing.

--ps 0 suppresses IID entirely, which flattens the image toward the bare mono downmix plus whatever ICC provides. It is a blunt instrument and mostly useful as a reference: encode once with it to hear what the core alone sounds like, then compare.

--ps 1 forces IID into the stream unconditionally. That is the setting that matters on material the heuristic misjudges, near equal level content above all, and it is also what carries the phase band count when you use IPD.

Values:

  • -1: stock. The loudness difference heuristic decides.
  • 0: IID never sent. Flattened, near mono image.
  • 1: IID always sent.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps 1 in.wav -o ps.m4a

Works with / against: --ps-ipd 1 makes the stream signal IID anyway, because that is the only route by which the phase band count reaches the decoder. --ps-iid-quant chooses the grid IID is quantised on. --ps-icc controls the other main parameter.

--ps-iid-quant <n>

Choose the coarse or the fine quantisation grid for the IID level differences.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: -1 default, 0 coarse, 1 fine
  • Input: whole number

IID is quantised onto a fixed grid of level differences. The fine grid resolves smaller level differences and therefore places sources more precisely, especially near the centre where small differences do most of the work. The coarse grid spends fewer bits per parameter and, on material whose image is wandering slightly from frame to frame, can actually sound steadier, because small analysis fluctuations quantise to the same step instead of dithering between two.

At the bitrates where PS is used, the parameter bits are a small part of the budget, so the fine grid is usually the right choice for music with real placement. Try coarse on very low rate speech or on material where you can hear the image jitter.

Values:

  • -1: stock grid for the configuration.
  • 0: coarse grid. Fewer parameter bits, blunter placement, steadier under jitter.
  • 1: fine grid. Finer placement, slightly more parameter bits.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps-iid-quant 1 in.wav -o fine.m4a

Works with / against: only meaningful when IID is actually sent, so pair with --ps 1 if the heuristic is suppressing it. Independent of --ps-bands, which decides how many IID values exist rather than how precisely each is coded.

--ps-icc <n>

Force the coherence parameter on or off.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: -1 auto (default), 0 off, 1 on
  • Input: whole number

ICC tells the decoder how much of a band should come from the decorrelated copy of the mono signal rather than from the mono signal itself. High coherence means a tight point source, low coherence means a wide diffuse one. On reverberant material it is ICC, not IID, that makes the difference between a room and a dry mono signal with a pan value.

--ps-icc 0 removes that control, so bands fall back to the coherent case and the result sounds drier and narrower, even when IID is still placing sources correctly. --ps-icc 1 forces coherence data into the stream where the encoder might have judged it unnecessary, which is the setting to try on ambient or heavily reverberant material whose width sounds collapsed.

Values:

  • -1: stock. The encoder decides.
  • 0: no ICC. Drier, narrower, point source feel.
  • 1: ICC always sent. Diffuse content keeps its width.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps-icc 1 in.wav -o icc.m4a

Works with / against: --ps-icc-mode changes only how the same matrix is signalled and is not a quality alternative to this switch. Complementary to --ps, which controls the level parameter.

--ps-ipd <0|1>

Transmit the inter-channel phase difference in the PS extension.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: 0 off (default, stock, phase never sent), 1 on
  • Input: whole number, on/off
