Without phase, PS positions sound using level differences and coherence alone. That covers anything panned by level, which is most of a modern mix, but it cannot represent a source placed by TIMING. A real inter-channel delay, the way low frequency localisation actually works in nature, is invisible to IID and ICC: both channels carry the same signal at the same level and differ only in when it arrives. Turning on IPD sends that angle, quantised onto the 8 defined steps of pi/4, in a length prefixed PS extension.

Where it helps is narrow and worth knowing exactly. IPD is coded for the lower 5 parameter bands in the 10 band configuration and the lower 11 in the 20 band configuration, which in frequency means roughly the bottom 690 Hz: those 11 bands live inside the first three QMF bands. Listen or measure above 690 Hz and you will find nothing at all. The content that benefits is low and low mid material, wide soft sources, reverb, and genuine inter-channel micro delays.

There is also a ceiling, and it is a property of the representation rather than a defect. A 45 degree grid can only track a delay while the phase it produces stays unambiguous across the coded range. By the top of the IPD range a delay of about 0.7 ms has already swung through nearly the full plus or minus 180 degrees, so the grid can no longer say where it is. This is the classic phase ambiguity limit of ITD, and the same reason hearing relies on timing cues mainly down low.

One practical coupling: the phase extension does not carry its own band count. The decoder derives that from the IID signalling, so enabling --ps-ipd also makes the stream signal IID. That is a cheap, always legal parameter and it is the only route by which the band count reaches the decoder.

Compatibility is safe by construction. The payload sits in a length prefixed extension, so a decoder without phase synthesis reads the byte count and skips it, which the standard explicitly permits. Note which decoders use it: the Fraunhofer decoder IGNORES the IPD extension, so decoding with it shows no change at all. ffmpeg does use it. Choose your decoder deliberately before concluding the switch did nothing.

Values:

  • 0: stock. No phase in the stream.
  • 1: IPD transmitted for the lower 5 (10 band) or 11 (20 band) parameter bands, and IID signalled along with it.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps-ipd 1 --ps-opd 1 in.wav -o ipd.m4a

Works with / against: --ps-opd is the other half of the phase pair and is only meaningful with this on. Forces IID signalling, so it overlaps --ps 1.

Measured, with ffmpeg as the decoder: probes built as identical signal in both channels, equal level, offset only by an inter-channel delay, comparing the decoded phase profile against the source inside the IPD range of 60 to 690 Hz.

Inter-channel delay phase error, IPD off phase error, IPD on
0.25 ms 35.0 deg 14.9 deg
0.4 ms 56.1 deg 24.9 deg
0.7 ms 67.4 deg 71.8 deg

At 0.25 and 0.4 ms the phase error more than halves, with identical figures at 32 and 48 kbps. At 0.7 ms the benefit is gone, which is the ambiguity ceiling described above.

--ps-opd <0|1>

Send the phase of the left channel relative to the mono downmix, the companion to IPD.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only, and only together with --ps-ipd 1
  • Values: 0 pins OPD to zero, 1 computes it (default behaviour once IPD is on)
  • Input: whole number, on/off

The distinction between the two phase parameters repays a careful read. IPD is the phase of the left channel relative to the right. OPD is the phase of the left channel relative to the mono downmix. The decoder needs both, because it rotates the paths fed from the downmix by OPD and the others by OPD minus IPD. Subtract those two and OPD cancels, so the inter-channel DIFFERENCE always comes out as IPD no matter what OPD says. What OPD decides is where that rotation sits relative to the downmix.

That cancellation is exactly why the problem is easy to miss. With OPD pinned to zero the left channel is glued to the downmix phase and the entire rotation is dumped onto the right one. Any measurement of arg(L) minus arg(R) is blind to this, because the difference is still correct. Measure each channel's absolute phase against the source instead, inside the IPD range, and the asymmetry is plain, as is OPD removing it.

Deriving OPD costs essentially nothing, which is the neat part. The downmix is L+R, so sum(L * conj(L+R)) equals sum(|L|^2) plus sum(L * conj(R)), whose real part is pwrL + pwrCr and whose imaginary part is pwrCi: the same accumulators already feeding IID, ICC and IPD. So OPD is atan2(pwrCi, pwrL + pwrCr), one more atan2 over numbers already in the loop, with no downmix signal needing to be passed in from elsewhere. For the equal level delay case this reduces analytically to IPD/2, since arg(1 + e^-i.phi) = -phi/2, which is a useful sanity check; the general form is also correct when the channels differ in level, where IPD/2 would not be.

Keep 0 for A/B comparison on identical material, and note that --ps-opd 0 on its own, without --ps-ipd 1, gives the stock stream, because there is no phase layer for it to modify. Note also that OPD differences only appear on material with real inter-channel delay; on level panned content there is nothing for it to correct.

Values:

  • 0: OPD pinned to zero. Phase error asymmetric between the channels.
  • 1: OPD computed from the existing accumulators. Symmetric phase error.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps-ipd 1 --ps-opd 0 in.wav -o opd0.m4a

Works with / against: meaningless without --ps-ipd 1. Does not change the inter-channel difference, only the absolute phase of each channel, so pick your measurement accordingly.

Measured, with ffmpeg as the decoder: absolute phase error of each channel against the source, inside the IPD range.

Inter-channel delay OPD = 0 (L / R) OPD computed (L / R)
0.25 ms 14.7 / 20.1 deg 14.4 / 17.3 deg
0.4 ms 23.0 / 28.0 deg 15.5 / 17.8 deg
0.7 ms 48.1 / 56.2 deg 40.9 / 36.3 deg

At OPD = 0 the two channels never agree, 14.7 against 20.1 and later 48.1 against 56.2. With OPD computed the pair converges, by +6.4 degrees on average and +13 at 0.7 ms. Note that 0.7 ms is the case where the plain IPD measurement showed no benefit: the inter-channel difference was already past what a 45 degree grid can resolve, yet the absolute phase of each channel still improved substantially.

--ps-icc-mode <n>

Select which of the two defined ICC rotation modes is signalled.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: -1 default, 0 ROT_A, 1 ROT_B
  • Input: whole number

Set expectations before you spend time here: this is signalling only. Both modes describe the same stereo matrix, the decoder simply derives it differently. Treat it as a compatibility knob rather than a quality one, and if you are hunting for audible stereo differences in PS, --ps-bands and --ps-env are where they actually live.

It is worth having because decoder implementations are not all equally exercised on both paths, so if a particular player renders a PS stream oddly, switching the rotation mode is a cheap thing to test.

Values:

  • -1: stock choice for the configuration.
  • 0: ROT_A signalled.
  • 1: ROT_B signalled.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps-icc-mode 1 in.wav -o rotb.m4a

Works with / against: only relevant when ICC is being sent, so check --ps-icc. Audibly far less significant than --ps-bands.

--ps-bands <10|20>

Frequency resolution of the stereo parameters: how many PS bands describe the image.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: 10 or 20; -1 picks from the bitrate (default), and from 22 kbps upward the table always gives 20
  • Input: whole number

This is one of the two axes that decide how many stereo parameters exist at all, and it is the frequency axis. Twenty bands describe the image in finer spectral detail, so a mix whose placement varies across the spectrum, bass centred, guitars wide, hats offset, keeps that structure. Ten bands average the same picture over twice the spectrum each.

The interesting case is the low end of the bitrate range. A coarse grid is not only cheaper, it can also sound steadier, because each parameter is averaged over more spectrum and therefore jitters less from frame to frame. A wandering image is more distracting than a slightly blurred one. Stock FDK derives the count from the bitrate alone and always picks 20 at 22 kbps and above, so without this switch you cannot audition the coarse grid at all on normal material.

Values:

  • 10: coarse grid. Fewer parameter bits, blurrier placement across frequency, steadier under analysis jitter.
  • 20: fine grid. Finer placement, more parameter bits.
  • -1: stock, picked from the bitrate.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 24000 --ps-bands 10 in.wav -o coarse.m4a

Works with / against: --ps-env is the time axis of the same idea. Both are audibly more significant than --ps-icc-mode. --ps-iid-quant changes the precision of each value rather than how many values there are.

--ps-env <1|2|4>

Time resolution of the stereo parameters: how many parameter envelopes per frame.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: 1, 2 or 4; -1 picks from the bitrate (default), and from 36 kbps upward the table gives 4
  • Input: whole number

The other resolution axis. One envelope means the whole frame gets a single stereo snapshot, four means the image is sampled four times within the frame. This is what decides whether a moving pan or a left/right transient keeps its position or smears across the frame into an average.

There is a trap, and it is the most important thing in this section: asking for four envelopes does not mean you get four. An automatic reduction loop keeps halving the count, four to two to one, whenever neighbouring envelopes look similar by a built-in error threshold, and on ordinary music that threshold is met very often. The request is undone before it reaches the stream. --ps-env-reduce 0 is what stops that, and the measurement under that card shows the difference plainly.

Values:

  • 1: one stereo snapshot per frame. Cheapest, smears any movement.
  • 2: two envelopes. A reasonable middle setting with --ps-env-reduce 0.
  • 4: four envelopes. Tracks moving panorama and transients closely.
  • -1: stock, picked from the bitrate.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps-env 4 --ps-env-reduce 0 in.wav -o env4.m4a

Works with / against: effectively requires --ps-env-reduce 0 to be honoured on ordinary material. --ps-bands is the frequency axis of the same tradeoff.

Measured: at HE-AAC v2 32 kbps --ps-env 4 does change the stream, because the bitrate table picks 2 there; at 48 kbps the table already gives 4, so the request matches stock and only --ps-env-reduce 0 makes a difference.

--ps-env-reduce <0|1>

Switch off the automatic envelope halving loop so the requested envelope count is the one transmitted.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: 0 disables the reduction loop, 1 leaves it on; -1 off, meaning stock, which has the loop on (default)
  • Input: whole number, on/off

The encoder keeps a reduction loop that compares neighbouring parameter envelopes and collapses four into two, and two into one, whenever they look similar enough by a built-in error threshold. The intent is to save parameter bits on static material. The side effect is that the envelope count you asked for is frequently not what gets transmitted, and because the saving is small while the cost is temporal resolution of the image, the trade is often a bad one on material with any movement at all.

--ps-env-reduce 0 turns the loop off and makes --ps-env literal. This is the single highest value setting in the PS group. The gain is in temporal accuracy of the stereo picture, at essentially the same file size, because parameter bits are a small fraction of the budget at these rates.

Values:

  • -1 or 1: stock. The loop runs and may collapse the envelope count.
  • 0: loop disabled, --ps-env taken literally.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 48000 --ps-env 4 --ps-env-reduce 0 in.wav -o literal.m4a

Works with / against: pointless without --ps-env, since its whole function is to protect that request. Combines freely with --ps-bands.

Measured: a 4 second stereo probe with a panorama swept at 0.25 Hz plus alternating left and right transients, HE-AAC v2 at 48 kbps, comparing the decoded panorama trajectory against the source.

Setting Panorama error (RMS) Correlation with source
stock (20 bands / 4 envelopes) 0.177 0.9655
--ps-env 2 --ps-env-reduce 0 0.138 0.9896
--ps-env 4 --ps-env-reduce 0 0.117 0.9944

Requesting 4 envelopes alone changes nothing at 48 kbps, the output is byte identical to stock, because the reduction loop undoes the request immediately. The gain appears only once the loop is stopped: a third less panorama error for roughly the same file size.

--ps-noenv-skip <0|1>

Forbid parameter-less PS frames, so every frame carries stereo information.

  • Applies to: HE-AAC v2 (-p 29) only
  • Values: 0 never emit parameter-less frames, 1 allow them; -1 off, meaning stock, which allows them (default)
  • Input: whole number, on/off

Beyond thinning out envelopes, the encoder can skip stereo parameters entirely for a stretch of frames. When successive IID and ICC sets look similar it may send up to ten consecutive frames carrying no stereo information at all, leaving the decoder to coast on the last values it received.

On static material that is free. On material where the image drifts slowly it is audible as the stereo picture flattening and then snapping back when parameters resume, because the decoder held a stale snapshot through the drift and then jumped to the current one. Once you know the artefact you tend to hear it, and it is more distracting than the bits it saves are worth.

--ps-noenv-skip 0 forbids the skipping and forces parameters into every frame. Use it whenever the image seems to breathe or stutter. The cost is a modest number of parameter bits per frame.

Values:

  • -1 or 1: stock. Up to ten consecutive frames may carry no stereo parameters.
  • 0: parameters in every frame.
fdkaac -p 29 -b 32000 --ps-noenv-skip 0 --ps-env 4 --ps-env-reduce 0 in.wav -o steady.m4a

Works with / against: the same family of hidden thinning as --ps-env-reduce, and the two are usually set together when the goal is a stable image. Neither changes the core audio, only the stereo parameter stream.

C. Bandwidth and SBR

Two different things decide how high your file sounds, and they are easy to confuse.

The first is the core bandwidth: the highest frequency the AAC core itself actually codes, with real spectral lines and real bits. Above that line the core sends nothing.

The second is the SBR crossover, also called the SBR start frequency. SBR (spectral band replication) does not code the high band. It copies lower, already coded spectrum upward and then describes, with a handful of parameters per frame, how that copy should be shaped. The crossover is where that copy begins. In a well formed HE-AAC stream the core bandwidth and the crossover sit at the same place: the core stops, SBR takes over, and the listener hears one continuous spectrum.

What SBR sends per frame is a short list:

  • envelopes: the level of each SBR band over time. More envelopes per frame means finer time resolution up top and more bits.
  • noise floor: how much synthetic noise to mix into each band. This is what turns a copied tonal region back into something that sounds like air, cymbals or breath.
  • inverse filtering level (invf): per band, how hard to whiten the copy. The copied material carries the tonal structure of the band it came from. If that structure does not belong up there, you hear it as a metallic, ringing, chorused top. Whitening flattens that structure out.
  • missing harmonics: flags that tell the decoder to insert a synthetic sine in a band where a real partial of the original got lost or landed in the wrong place.

The last two are decided by estimators inside the encoder, which compare the tonality of the original high band with the tonality of the patched copy, band by band and frame by frame. The estimators are where the character of the top end is set, and they trade off against each other in one direction: too little whitening and too little fill noise gives a metallic, ringing, phasey top; too much of both gives a hissy, washed out top where cymbals turn into shaped noise and sustained partials disappear. Most of the knobs in this group move you along exactly that axis. There is no universally correct point on it; it depends on the material and on the bitrate.

SBR is active in these profiles:

  • -p 5 HE-AAC
  • -p 29 HE-AAC v2 (SBR plus parametric stereo)
  • -p 39 -L 1 AAC-ELD with SBR

In -p 2 (AAC-LC), -p 23 (AAC-LD) and -p 39 without -L 1 there is no SBR, and every --sbr-* switch in this group does nothing. --core-cutoff works everywhere.

Two conventions used throughout this group:

  • x100 knobs scale an internal constant: 100 means 1.00 and is exactly stock, below 100 makes the constant smaller, above 100 makes it larger. Both directions work. A few knobs are documented as raw x100, which means the value replaces the constant outright rather than scaling it (--sbr-tran-peak 90 is the stock 0.90).
  • --verbose prints the effective core bandwidth, crossover, final top edge and SBR header period. Under SBR it is the only reliable way to see what you actually got, and several switches in this group are validated by FDK only at initialisation time, so checking is not optional.

Core bandwidth: --core-cutoff

--core-cutoff <hz>

Forces the upper limit of the band the AAC core codes, in Hz, and it keeps working under SBR where -w does not.

  • Applies to: every profile. This is the one switch in group C that is useful without SBR.
  • Values: 0..48000 Hz (out of range is rejected); the encoder stops at the core Nyquist (half the core sample rate); default 0 = leave FDK's own decision
  • Input: whole number

The standard -w/--bandwidth is overridden when SBR initialises: the SBR tuning table decides the core bandwidth, so that the core stops exactly where the crossover begins, and whatever you asked for with -w is discarded. --core-cutoff is applied after that and survives it.

Two situations make this worth reaching for. The first is SBR material where you want more real coded spectrum under the crossover than the table gives you, typically because the patched band sounds synthetic and you would rather spend bits on genuine mid treble. The second has nothing to do with SBR: AAC-LD and AAC-ELD pick their core bandwidth from a table that is very conservative, about 2 kHz at 48 kbps and about 5.8 kHz at 64 kbps. Those profiles sound muffled for that reason alone, and --core-cutoff is the fix.

The encoder cannot cut at an arbitrary Hz value. The spectrum is divided into scalefactor bands, so the cutoff snaps to the nearest SFB boundary, and --verbose reports the real, SFB anchored result. Asking for 17300 Hz and being told 17915 Hz is not an error; 17915 Hz is what is in the stream.

Mind the ceiling. The maximum is half the core sample rate, and with dual rate SBR the core runs at half the input rate: for 48 kHz input under HE-AAC the core runs at 24 kHz, so the hard ceiling is 12 kHz. There is also a separate 20 kHz cap that applies without SBR; --uncap-bandwidth (group A) lifts it so that --core-cutoff can reach up to half the sample rate on high rate material, and that flag does nothing on its own.

You police the result yourself. Pushing the core cutoff far below the crossover leaves everything between the core top and the SBR start with nothing in it, which is audible as a gap in the mid treble rather than as a gentle rolloff. If you want a genuinely low core, move the crossover down with --sbr-start so that the two meet again.

Examples, narrowing the core (HE-AAC -p 5, 64 kbps stereo, 48 kHz input, where the table gives about 10500 Hz of core):

  • Mild: --core-cutoff 9000. A slightly cheaper core, a slightly wider SBR region. Bits freed up in the core go to the lower spectrum.
  • Strong: --core-cutoff 7500. Clearly more of the spectrum is now replicated rather than coded. Expect a thinner, more synthetic 8..10 kHz region, with a cleaner low mid.
  • Exaggerated: --core-cutoff 2000. The core ends at the top of the vocal fundamentals. Everything from 2 kHz to the crossover is a hole. A caricature unless you also pull --sbr-start right down.

Examples, widening the core (same conditions):

  • Mild: --core-cutoff 11500. A little real spectrum above the usual crossover.
  • Strong: --core-cutoff 12000 at 48 kHz input under HE-AAC. The core now runs to its Nyquist. Real coded treble, but the core pays full price for a band SBR was already covering for free, so at 64 kbps the rest of the spectrum gets coarser.
  • Exaggerated: --core-cutoff 20000 on AAC-LC at 320 kbps, with -p 2 and no SBR at all. Legal and sometimes genuinely wanted, but at low bitrate the same request would drain the bit budget into inaudible treble.

Works with / against: under SBR, -w is ignored, and --verbose still labels the automatic value "[from -w]" even though --core-cutoff is what set it. Checking the reported number rather than the label is the habit to build. Pair with --sbr-start when you move the cutoff far, so the core and the crossover still meet. --uncap-bandwidth is needed only to go past 20 kHz without SBR.

Measured: --core-cutoff 7500 under HE-AAC v2 at 48 kbps gives an effective 7500 Hz core, where stock -w 7500 leaves 8613 Hz because it is ignored.

SBR frequency layout: --sbr-start --sbr-stop --sbr-freqscale --sbr-alterscale --sbr-noise-bands --sbr-amp-res --sbr-data-extra

These seven switches define the shape of the SBR band before anything is measured in it: where the replicated region starts, where it ends, how it is divided into bands, how finely the noise floor is described, and how precisely envelope levels are quantised.

The two important ones are --sbr-start and --sbr-stop. Both take a table index, not a frequency, and the frequency an index maps to depends on the sample rate, the profile and the bitrate. Measured values are given in the cards below; read them as a guide to the direction and the spacing, and confirm the actual numbers for your settings with --verbose.

The rule that catches everyone: legality depends on the pair, the bitrate and the sample rate. SBR requires a valid number of master bands between start and stop, and FDK validates the combination when the encoder initialises. A bad pair does not degrade gracefully; it reports "encoder initialization failed" and nothing is written. The legal combinations form a diagonal band: as you raise the start you must usually raise the stop with it. At HE-AAC 64 kbps stereo, 48 kHz input, every start from 0 to 4 is illegal with every stop, and the documented looking pair --sbr-start 5 --sbr-stop 9 fails there. Pairs that do work at that setting: 8/9, 8/14, 14/12.

So: change one index at a time, run the encode, and read --verbose. An encode that fails to initialise costs you a second; a stream with a crossover you did not intend costs you the listening session.

--sbr-start <n>

Sets where the core stops and the replicated high band begins.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only (-p 5, -p 29, -p 39 -L 1)
  • Values: table index 0..15; default -1 = the value from the SBR tuning table
  • Input: whole number

This is the most consequential single number in an HE-AAC stream. Everything below the crossover is real coded spectrum; everything above it is a described copy. Lowering the start gives the core less to do, so what it does code it codes more cleanly, and a larger share of the spectrum becomes parametric. Raising the start does the opposite: more real treble, a smaller SBR region, and a core that has to pay for it.

At very low bitrate, lowering the crossover is often the only way to make the core sound clean at all. At 64 kbps and above, raising it tends to win, because the core can afford genuine 10..12 kHz detail and a copied band always carries some of the character of the band it was copied from.

Measured core bandwidth per start index, as printed by --verbose at HE-AAC -p 5, 64 kbps, 48 kHz stereo input, with the default stop. "Illegal" means encoder initialisation failed for every stop tried:

--sbr-start core bandwidth
0..4 illegal at this setting
5 5062 Hz (only with --sbr-stop 0)
6 5484 Hz
7 5953 Hz
8 6468 Hz
9 6468 Hz
10 7031 Hz
11 7640 Hz
12 8250 Hz
13 9000 Hz
14 10500 Hz (stock at this bitrate)
15 12000 Hz

The same sweep at 44.1 kHz input, HE-AAC 32 kbps: start 5 gives 5038 Hz, 8 gives 5943 Hz, 11 gives 7579 Hz, 14 gives 9646 Hz, 15 gives 11025 Hz. Note that index 8 and 9 land on the same core bandwidth at 48 kHz: the mapping is a table, not a formula, and neighbouring indices can coincide.

Examples, lowering the crossover (HE-AAC 64 kbps stereo, 48 kHz, stock 14):

  • Mild: --sbr-start 13. Core to 9000 Hz. Marginally cleaner core, slightly wider patch.
  • Strong: --sbr-start 11. Core to 7640 Hz. Noticeably more of the top is parametric. Worth trying when the core sounds grainy.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-start 6 --sbr-stop 9. Core to 5484 Hz. Almost everything above the presence region is a copy; cymbals and sibilants are entirely SBR's invention. This is the right shape at 24 kbps and a caricature at 64.

Examples, raising the crossover:

  • Mild: --sbr-start 15. Core to 12000 Hz, the top of the index range at this rate. Real coded spectrum up to 12 kHz, a narrower SBR region above it.
  • Strong: raising the crossover further is not available through this switch at this setting, because 15 is the highest index. The next step is to drop SBR and code the whole band with -p 2 plus -w.
  • Exaggerated: the same, taken to its conclusion: -p 2 -b 64000 -w 16000. No SBR, no copy, and at 64 kbps stereo a core that is clearly short of bits for a 16 kHz band. Instructive to compare against HE-AAC on the same material.

Works with / against: must form a legal pair with --sbr-stop. --core-cutoff can move the core bandwidth independently of the crossover, which is how you create a gap if you are not careful. Lowering the start widens the band that --sbr-noise-bands, --sbr-invf* and the missing harmonics detector are responsible for, so their settings matter more the lower you go.

--sbr-stop <n>

Sets the top edge of the replicated band, which is the top edge of the file.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: table index 0..13; default -1 = the value from the SBR tuning table
  • Input: whole number

Where --sbr-start splits the spectrum, this sets the ceiling. Raising it extends the patched region higher, which costs almost nothing in bits (SBR describes bands, not lines) but spreads the same parameter budget over a wider region, so each band is described more coarsely. Lowering it closes the file off earlier, which sounds like a conventional lowpass and sometimes reads as cleaner, because a mediocre 18..22 kHz patch contributes more hiss than content.

Measured final top edge of the decoded output (core plus SBR) per stop index, --verbose at HE-AAC -p 5, 64 kbps, 48 kHz stereo input, with --sbr-start 14:

--sbr-stop final top edge
0..3 illegal at this setting
4 about 10875 Hz
5 about 12000 Hz
6 about 13125 Hz
7 about 14250 Hz
8 about 15375 Hz
9 about 16875 Hz
10 about 18375 Hz
11 about 20250 Hz
12 about 22125 Hz (stock)
13 illegal at this setting

Examples, lowering the top edge (from stock 12):

  • Mild: --sbr-start 14 --sbr-stop 11. Top at about 20250 Hz. Hard to hear, slightly denser description of what remains.
  • Strong: --sbr-start 14 --sbr-stop 9. Top at about 16875 Hz. A deliberate, audible ceiling, with the remaining bands described more finely. Often the better trade at 32 kbps and below.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-start 14 --sbr-stop 4. Top at about 10875 Hz with a 10500 Hz core: SBR now covers a few hundred Hz and contributes nothing. You have effectively built an AAC-LC stream with SBR overhead attached.

Examples, raising the top edge:

  • Mild: none upward at this setting. Stop 12 (about 22125 Hz) is already the stock top and stop 13 is not legal with start 14. The top can be held while the crossover moves up: --sbr-start 15 --sbr-stop 12 keeps about 22125 Hz with a 12000 Hz core.
  • Strong: --sbr-start 8 --sbr-stop 14. A wide patched region from 6468 Hz up. Cheap in bits, coarse in description: watch for a "shimmering" quality on dense material.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-stop 13 at this setting, which fails to initialise. The index exists in the standard but not in every legal pair, and this is the fastest way to see what the diagonal legality band means in practice.

Works with / against: the pair constraint with --sbr-start governs both. Index 13 is accepted by the parser but is not legal in every pair, which is why --verbose and a test encode come before any listening.

--sbr-freqscale <n>

Chooses how the replicated band is divided into SBR bands: linearly or on one of three logarithmic grids.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: 0 = linear, 1, 2, 3 = logarithmic with 12, 10 and 8 bands per octave (progressively coarser); -1 or leaving the switch out = the value from the SBR tuning table
  • Input: whole number

SBR describes its region band by band, and this picks the ruler. A linear grid gives every band the same width in Hz, so the top octave gets many bands and the bottom of the SBR region gets few. A logarithmic grid spaces bands by ratio, which matches hearing: more resolution where the ear resolves more, down near the crossover.

Values:

  • 0 linear. Even spacing in Hz. Fine resolution high up, coarse just above the crossover, which is the opposite of what the ear wants. Occasionally useful on material whose high band is genuinely uniform, such as broadband noise beds.
  • 1 finest logarithmic grouping, 12 bands per octave. Most bands, most parameters, best shaping of an uneven high band.
  • 2 middle logarithmic grouping, 10 bands per octave.
  • 3 coarsest logarithmic grouping, 8 bands per octave. Fewest bands, cheapest description, least control.

Measured at HE-AAC 64 kbps, SBR payload per frame: 220 bits at 0, 188 at 1, 170 at 2, 152 at 3.

  • -1 off: the tuning table decides, which is the right choice unless you have a reason.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-freqscale 1 -o out.m4a in.wav (finest grid)

Works with / against: the number of bands this produces interacts with --sbr-noise-bands (noise bands are derived per octave, not per SBR band) and with how much the invf decision can vary across the region. A finer grid means a finer invf map.

--sbr-alterscale <n>

Switches to the alternative band grouping resolution, a secondary adjustment to the grid chosen by --sbr-freqscale.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: 0 = off, 1 = on; default -1 = the value from the SBR tuning table
  • Input: whole number

This is a standard SBR header flag that slightly changes how the master band table is derived, which in practice moves a few band edges. It is a refinement, not a character control: there is no listening result here worth promising in either position, and nothing in our measurements isolates it.

Values:

  • 0 the alternative resolution off.
  • 1 the alternative resolution on.
  • -1 off: follow the tuning table. Recommended.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-freqscale 2 --sbr-alterscale 1 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: only meaningful together with --sbr-freqscale, since it adjusts that grid. If you are hunting for a tonal change up top, --sbr-invf* and --sbr-noise-bands are the switches with real leverage; reach for this one last.

--sbr-noise-bands <n>

Sets how finely the SBR noise floor is described across frequency.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: 0, 1, 2, 3; default -1 = the value from the SBR tuning table
  • Input: whole number

The noise floor is how SBR puts "air" back into a copied band. With one noise band for the whole region, the encoder sends a single noise level and the decoder spreads it evenly: cheap, and wrong whenever the high band is not uniform, for example a sustained string note under a hi-hat. With more noise bands, the level can follow the material, so a quiet tonal region does not get the same hiss as a busy one.

The field in the SBR header is two bits wide, so there are exactly four settings and no more. The values are not a literal band count: they are a density, roughly bands per octave.

Values:

  • 0 one noise band for the entire SBR region. Cheapest, bluntest. At very low bitrate the saving is real; on anything with structure up top you hear noise in the wrong places.
  • 1 about one noise band per octave.
  • 2 about two per octave.
  • 3 about three per octave. Densest description, best separation of tonal and noisy regions, most side information.
  • -1 off: the tuning table decides by bitrate.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 96000 --sbr-noise-bands 3 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: this decides the resolution of the noise floor; --sbr-noise-max, --sbr-nf-weight and --sbr-noise-floor-offset decide its level. Raising the density is wasted if the level is clipped at a low ceiling, so if you are chasing air, look at --sbr-noise-max first.

--sbr-amp-res <n>

Chooses the quantisation step of the SBR envelope levels: 1.5 dB or 3.0 dB.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: 0 = 1.5 dB, 1 = 3.0 dB; default -1 = the value from the SBR tuning table
  • Input: whole number

Envelope levels are sent as quantised numbers, and this is the step size. A 1.5 dB step tracks level changes in the high band more exactly and costs more bits per envelope. A 3.0 dB step is cheaper and coarser; on material with strong level movement up top, the coarse step is audible as the high band stepping rather than gliding.

Values:

  • 0 1.5 dB steps. Finer, more expensive. Prefer at higher bitrate or on dynamic material.
  • 1 3.0 dB steps. Coarser, cheaper. The sensible trade when bits are scarce, which is why the table often picks it low down.
  • -1 off: the tuning table decides.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 80000 --sbr-amp-res 0 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: interacts directly with --sbr-num-env and the transient detector, because every extra envelope costs a full set of levels at whatever step size is in force. Four envelopes at 1.5 dB resolution is the expensive corner of this group.

--sbr-data-extra <n>

Writes the optional extra SBR header fields instead of omitting them.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: 0 = do not write, 1 = write; default -1 = the value from the SBR tuning table
  • Input: whole number

The SBR header has an optional block carrying additional configuration. Writing it makes every header slightly larger and the configuration more explicit; omitting it leaves the decoder on default assumptions for those fields. This is a bitstream formatting choice, not a sound control, and it is only worth touching when you are chasing compatibility with a specific decoder or inspecting headers.

Values:

  • 0 do not write the extra header data.
  • 1 write it.
  • -1 off: follow the tuning table.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-data-extra 1 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: combined with a short --sbr-header-period the extra bytes repeat often, so if you are optimising a low bitrate stream for fast SBR lock, keep this off.

SBR time resolution and stereo: --sbr-num-env --sbr-freqres-fixfix --sbr-stereo-mode --sbr-header-period --sbr-header-time

Frequency layout decides where the SBR band is. This section decides how often it is described, how it is split between channels, and how often the configuration itself is repeated in the stream.

Time resolution is a direct bits versus smearing trade. One envelope per frame means one level per band for the whole frame, so a hi-hat inside that frame is averaged with the silence around it. Four envelopes track the shape but quadruple the envelope data. The transient detector normally makes this decision per frame, which is why forcing a fixed count is a bigger intervention than it looks.

The header period is a different kind of setting: it does not change how the file sounds once playback is running, it changes how long a decoder that joins mid stream waits before it has any high band at all. It matters for live streaming and for nothing else.

--sbr-num-env <1|2|4>

Forces a fixed number of SBR envelopes per frame and switches off the transient detector's framing decision.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: 1, 2, 4; default -1 = off, the transient detector decides per frame
  • Input: whole number

Normally SBR uses a variable time grid: frames with a detected attack get more, shorter envelopes so the attack is not smeared, and steady frames get one. This switch replaces that with a static grid, every frame the same, regardless of content.

That makes it a good tool for stable material and a bad one for percussive material. On a sustained pad, organ or string bed, forcing 2 or 4 envelopes buys you time resolution the detector would not have asked for, and the cost is predictable. On drums, the static grid means attacks no longer get their own envelope, and you get exactly the backwards smearing the detector exists to prevent.

The value 8 would exceed the standard's grid and is rejected.

Values:

  • 1 one envelope per frame. Cheapest, bluntest; the high band holds one level per band for the whole frame. Expect audible smearing on anything transient.
  • 2 two envelopes per frame. A middle ground, and a reasonable forced setting on steady material at low bitrate.
  • 4 four envelopes per frame. Finest static resolution, most envelope data. Good on sustained, ringing sounds; on sharp attacks it still loses to the dynamic grid, because the splits are in fixed places rather than at the attack.
  • -1 off. The transient detector picks per frame. This is the better default, and the knobs to shape it are in the transient detector section below.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-num-env 4 -o out.m4a organ.wav

Works with / against: this overrides the framing part of what --sbr-tran-thr and --sbr-tran-split influence, so combining them is contradictory: if you want better behaviour on attacks, leave --sbr-num-env off and lower --sbr-tran-thr instead. The bit cost per envelope is set by --sbr-amp-res.

--sbr-freqres-fixfix <0|1>

Sets the frequency resolution of the FIXFIX envelope, the envelope type used in frames with no detected transient.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only
  • Values: 0 = low, 1 = high; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

An envelope can describe the high band with few bands or with many. FIXFIX is the envelope shape used for ordinary, transient free frames, which on most material is the large majority of them, so this quietly affects most of the file.

Low resolution groups bands together: fewer levels per envelope, cheaper, and less able to follow an uneven high band. High resolution sends more levels, tracks spectral shape better, and costs bits that at low bitrate come out of the core.

Values:

  • 0 low frequency resolution. Cheaper; the high band is described more coarsely across frequency. Sensible at low bitrate and on uniform high bands.
  • 1 high frequency resolution. More levels per envelope, better shaping of structured high bands. Sensible when bits are available.
  • -1 off: the encoder's own choice, which follows the tuning table and the frame type.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 96000 --sbr-freqres-fixfix 1 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: this is the frequency axis; --sbr-num-env is the time axis. Both multiply into the same envelope data budget, and --sbr-amp-res sets the price per level. It applies only to transient free frames, so on heavily percussive material its share of the file is smaller.

--sbr-stereo-mode <0..3>

Chooses how the SBR high band is shared between the two channels, independently of the core's stereo coding.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only, stereo input
  • Values: 0 mono, 1 LR, 2 coupling, 3 switch-LRC; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

The core and SBR make separate stereo decisions. The core may be coding mid/side while SBR is coupling, or the reverse. This switch addresses only the high band.

Full left/right separation sends an independent set of SBR parameters per channel: the widest, most faithful top end, at roughly double the SBR side information. Coupling sends one shared envelope plus level information, which is much cheaper and collapses the stereo image up top towards the centre. On most material that collapse is less audible than you would expect, which is why it is the normal choice at low bitrate; on wide, hard panned cymbals and on recordings with real room difference between channels, it is clearly audible.

Values:

  • 0 mono. One high band for both channels. Only sensible on genuinely mono content, or as a deliberate caricature to hear what the other modes buy you.
  • 1 LR, full separation. Independent high band per channel. The audiophile setting at higher bitrate; the expensive one at 32 kbps.
  • 2 coupling. Shared envelope plus level and balance. Cheap, slightly narrower top.
  • 3 switch-LRC. The encoder decides per frame, which is the sensible general case and what the default behaviour amounts to.
  • -1 off: the encoder decides, as in 3.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 96000 --sbr-stereo-mode 1 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: in -p 29 (HE-AAC v2) parametric stereo is also describing the image, so forcing LR here fights the premise of that profile; at HE-AAC v2 bitrates the bits are better spent elsewhere. Core stereo switches in group B are a separate decision and do not override this one.

--sbr-header-period <n>

Sets how many frames apart SBR headers are written, which decides how long a decoder joining a live stream hears core only audio before the high band appears.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only. In HE-AAC and HE-AAC v2 it controls the in-band sbr_header; in AAC-ELD with SBR there is no in-band SBR header, so the period applies to the LATM/LOAS StreamMuxConfig and therefore needs -f 6, -f 7 or -f 10
  • Values: frames, 1 and up; default -1 = off, FDK's own period of about 10 frames (about 0.46 s at 44.1 kHz)
  • Input: whole number

The entire SBR configuration lives in a periodic header, not in every frame. A listener who tunes into an Icecast, Shoutcast or HLS stream mid stream decodes the core immediately and hears a muffled, lowpassed version of the programme until the next SBR header arrives. Then the top end appears. On a file this is invisible, since playback starts at the beginning; on a live stream it is the first thing a new listener hears.

Writing the header in every frame removes that moment almost entirely, at the cost of the header bytes in every frame. At low bitrate those bytes are not nothing. Stretching the period saves them and lengthens the core only moment.

The value is taken literally, with no one second ceiling applied.

Examples, shortening the period:

  • Mild: --sbr-header-period 5. Roughly halves the wait against the default.
  • Strong: --sbr-header-period 2. A header every other frame; the core only moment is down to tens of milliseconds.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-header-period 1. A header in every single frame, near instant SBR lock, maximum overhead. On a 24 kbps stream that overhead is a real share of the budget, which is why it is a caricature there and a reasonable choice at 96 kbps.

Examples, lengthening the period:

  • Mild: --sbr-header-period 25. About a second between headers at 44.1 kHz. Barely noticeable on a stream people stay tuned to.
  • Strong: --sbr-header-period 100. Several seconds of core only audio for a new listener. Saves header bytes, sounds broken to anyone channel hopping.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-header-period 5000. Minutes between headers. For a file this is harmless; for a stream it means most listeners never hear the high band at all.

Works with / against: --sbr-header-time expresses the same thing in seconds and wins when both are given. --sbr-data-extra 1 makes each header bigger, so the two together multiply the overhead. --verbose prints the effective period in frames and milliseconds.

--sbr-header-time <s>

The same setting as --sbr-header-period, expressed in seconds instead of frames.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles only; in AAC-ELD with SBR it sets the LATM/LOAS StreamMuxConfig period, so use -f 6, -f 7 or -f 10
  • Values: 0.01..600 seconds; default -1 = off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

Frames are an awkward unit when a stream operator specifies a requirement, because the frame duration depends on the sample rate and the profile. This switch takes the seconds directly and converts them for you, so the same command line behaves the same way at 44.1 and 48 kHz.

It wins over --sbr-header-period when both are given.

With AAC-ELD in ADTS or M4A there is nothing to repeat: the configuration sits only in the container, and --verbose reports the SBR header period as the LATM/LOAS config period rather than as an in-band SBR header.

Examples, shortening the time:

  • Mild: --sbr-header-time 0.25. A quarter second worst case wait.
  • Strong: --sbr-header-time 0.05. Effectively immediate lock for a listener joining at random.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-header-time 0.01, the minimum. At most sample rates this is shorter than a frame, so it amounts to a header in every frame with the maximum overhead that implies.

Examples, lengthening the time:

  • Mild: --sbr-header-time 1. One second, close to a common operator requirement.
  • Strong: --sbr-header-time 3.5. Three and a half seconds of core only audio for a new listener. Measured on the decoder side with this setting, the longest observed wait for the high band after joining mid stream was 3.41 s for HE-AAC, 3.46 s for HE-AAC v2 and 3.52 s for AAC-ELD.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-header-time 600, the maximum, ten minutes. Legal, and for a live stream indistinguishable from shipping a core only stream.

Works with / against: overrides --sbr-header-period. Check the result in --verbose, which prints the effective period both ways. Both . and , are accepted as the decimal separator.

SBR transient detector: --sbr-tran-peak --sbr-tran-quiet --sbr-tran-dom --sbr-tran-thr --sbr-tran-split

SBR divides each frame into time slots and decides, from the energy in those slots, whether something in the high band attacked. If it says yes, the attack gets its own short envelope and the level jump is coded where it happens. If it says no, one envelope covers the whole stretch and the energy of the hit is averaged across it.

Averaging across an attack is audible, and it is audible before the attack, not after: you hear a short burst of high frequency hiss just ahead of the hi-hat or the clap. That is pre-echo, and in the replicated band it is one of the more recognisable coding artefacts, because the material up there is already synthetic enough that a smear reads as noise rather than as a soft attack.

Five knobs reach into this detector. One of them, --sbr-tran-thr, is far more effective than the rest and is the one to try first. Two of them, --sbr-tran-quiet and --sbr-tran-dom, live in a second, faster detector that only runs for AAC-ELD with SBR, so they do nothing in HE-AAC and HE-AAC v2.

All five are x100 knobs and all of them work in both directions: 100 means the stock constant exactly, below 100 makes the constant smaller, above 100 makes it larger. --sbr-tran-peak is the exception in meaning, not in format: it replaces the constant rather than scaling it.

--sbr-tran-thr <n>

Scales the master threshold that decides how readily the SBR transient detector fires at all, and it is the one knob in this section with a large, measured effect.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles (-p 5, -p 29, -p 39 -L 1)
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

The detector compares slot energies against a threshold derived from the signal. Lower the threshold and marginal attacks that previously went unnoticed now get their own envelope; raise it and only the most obvious hits do. Because missing an attack is what produces pre-echo, lowering this is the direct cure, and the cost is envelope data: more detected transients means more envelopes means more bits, taken from the core.

This is the knob that justifies the group. On percussive material it removes high band pre-echo almost entirely.

Measured, pre-echo in the SBR band. Test signal: 24 percussive attacks of hi-hat and clap character plus four decaying tones between 8 and 13 kHz. Metric: energy above 9 kHz appearing in the two 5.8 ms windows before each attack that is absent from the source, so lower is better. Encoded as HE-AAC, decoded, lag aligned by cross correlation first:

--sbr-tran-thr pre-echo at 32 kbps pre-echo at 64 kbps
stock 1.610 1.186
20 / 40 / 60 0.041 0.059
80 0.661 0.502
100 and above 1.610 (= stock) 1.186 (= stock)

The effect is monotonic, reproducible at both bitrates and saturated below 60. Values of 150 and 400 do change the stream, in the other direction.

Examples, lowering the threshold (detector fires more readily):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-thr 80. Pre-echo roughly cut in half on the measured signal, modest extra envelope cost.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-thr 40. Pre-echo essentially gone on percussive material. This is the recommended starting point when you hear smearing up top.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-thr 1. The detector fires on almost everything. Below 60 there is nothing further to gain in pre-echo, so all you buy is envelope data spent on non events, which at low bitrate takes bits off the core and makes the whole file worse.

Examples, raising the threshold (detector fires less readily):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-thr 150. Marginal attacks are ignored, slightly fewer envelopes, slightly more smearing. Occasionally worth it on steady material at a very tight budget.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-thr 400. Only unmistakable attacks get their own envelope. Expect clearly more pre-echo on drums.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-thr 10000. The detector effectively never fires; every frame is described with the static grid. A caricature, and a useful one if you want to hear the artefact this detector exists to prevent.

Works with / against: --sbr-num-env forces a static grid and so removes the framing benefit this knob buys, which makes combining them self defeating. --sbr-tran-split handles the separate question of splitting frames that have no detected transient at all. For pre-echo in the core (LC, LD, ELD), the tools are in group E.

--sbr-tran-split <n>

Scales the threshold for splitting a frame that has no detected transient into two envelopes anyway.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

Not every level change in the high band is an attack. A crescendo, a swell, a chord change or a cymbal decaying unevenly all move the high band without triggering the transient detector, and one envelope for the whole frame then averages the movement away. This threshold decides how readily such a frame is still split in two, giving the second half its own levels.

Lowering it splits more often: finer time resolution on material that moves but does not attack, at the cost of a second set of envelope levels in many frames. Raising it splits less often: cheaper, and the high band follows slow movement less closely.

This is a smaller effect than --sbr-tran-thr, and it addresses a different symptom. If the problem is hiss before a hit, use the threshold; if the problem is a high band that sounds static while the music swells, use this.

Examples, lowering (split more often):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-split 70. Slightly more frames get two envelopes.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-split 30. Most frames with any movement get split. Noticeably more envelope data, better tracking of swells.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-split 1. Nearly every frame split regardless of content; the bit cost is real and the benefit on steady material is zero.

Examples, raising (split less often):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-split 150. Marginally fewer splits, marginally cheaper.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-split 300. Splits become rare. Measured: this does change the HE-AAC stream.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-split 10000. No frame is ever split on movement alone. One envelope per non transient frame, maximum smearing of slow high band changes, minimum envelope data.

Works with / against: ignored in effect when --sbr-num-env forces a static grid. Complements --sbr-tran-thr rather than duplicating it: that one is about attacks, this one about frames with no attack. Every extra envelope is priced by --sbr-amp-res.

--sbr-tran-peak <n>

Sets how peaky a candidate must be before it counts as an attack: the signal must fall below this fraction of the previous slot.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..200, raw x100 where 90 = the stock 0.90; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

A real attack is not just loud, it is brief: the energy rises and then drops again. The detector checks that drop, requiring the following slot to fall below a fraction of the previous one. That fraction is this constant.

Note the unit carefully. This is a raw x100 value, not a scale factor: it replaces the constant outright, so 90 is the stock 0.90 and is a no-op, while 100 is 1.0, not "stock". And 1.0 has a special consequence: a fraction of 1.0 or more means the condition can never be satisfied by a genuine drop in the way the detector tests it, so a slot never counts as dropping. Every value from 100 to 200 therefore produces one identical stream. The live range is 1..99.

Lowering the value demands a sharper drop, so fewer candidates qualify as attacks. Raising it toward 99 accepts gentler decays as attacks, so more do.

Examples, lowering (stricter, fewer transients):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-peak 80. Slightly stricter than stock; soft attacks stop qualifying.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-peak 50. Only sharply decaying hits count. Expect more smearing on anything but dry percussion.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-peak 1. The drop requirement is absurd; almost nothing qualifies as an attack and the peak test alone suppresses the detector.

Examples, raising (looser, more transients):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-peak 95. Gentler decays now count as attacks.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-peak 99. The loosest setting that still does anything. Attacks with long tails get their own envelopes.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-peak 200. Beyond the useful range: 100 and above all mean 1.0, a slot never counts as dropping, and 100 through 200 are one and the same stream. Useful only as a demonstration that the knob has a hard edge.

Works with / against: this is one condition of several; --sbr-tran-thr scales the master threshold and has far more leverage. If you want more or fewer transients overall, start there and use this only to change the character of what qualifies.

--sbr-tran-quiet <n>

Sets the quiet slot energy floor of the fast SBR transient detector, which decides how readily quiet passages produce transients.

  • Applies to: AAC-ELD with SBR only (-p 39 -L 1). No effect in HE-AAC or HE-AAC v2, which use the other detector
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

AAC-ELD with SBR runs a second, lower latency transient detector, and that detector has an energy floor below which a slot is treated as quiet. The floor exists so that noise in silence does not register as attacks. Lower it and the detector looks into quieter passages, so soft hits in a sparse arrangement can get their own envelope. Raise it and quiet material is ignored.

Behaviour is strongly material dependent, which is worth knowing before you spend time on it: on a purely tonal test signal the detector never reached a decision this knob could change.

Measured at AAC-ELD with SBR, 24 kbps, across five test signals: values from 30 to 99 change the stream, 100 and above gave no change at all, and 1 and 10 give the same stream as each other. So the live range here is roughly 11..99 downward, with a floor effect below that and a ceiling at 100.

Examples, lowering (quiet passages produce transients more readily):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-quiet 90. A small extension into quieter slots.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-quiet 50. Clearly more sensitive in sparse material; measured to change the stream.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-quiet 1. Floored: 1 and 10 give the same stream, so the detector has hit the bottom of what the floor can express.

Examples, raising (quiet passages ignored):

  • Mild through exaggerated: --sbr-tran-quiet 120, 300, 10000 all gave no stream change in our measurements at ELD plus SBR 24 kbps. The stock floor is already high enough that raising it further changes no decision on this material. Honest status: the direction is wired, the effect was not observed upward.

Works with / against: nothing in HE-AAC or HE-AAC v2, because the fast detector is not used there. Within ELD plus SBR it sits alongside --sbr-tran-dom; --sbr-tran-thr and --sbr-tran-split apply to the main detector.

--sbr-tran-dom <n>

Sets how much louder a slot must be than the two before it when those are already marked as transients.

  • Applies to: AAC-ELD with SBR only (-p 39 -L 1). No effect in HE-AAC or HE-AAC v2
  • Values: 100..500, x100 where 140 = the stock 1.4; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

A roll, a flam or a double hit produces several candidate slots in a row. This rule stops the detector from marking all of them: once the two preceding slots are already transients, the next one has to be clearly louder, by this factor, to be marked as well. It is a dominance test, which is where the name comes from.

Lowering the factor toward 100 lets consecutive slots all count, so dense percussion produces more transients. Raising it means only a decisive escalation counts, so a roll registers once.

Honest status: this knob is wired to the constant it documents, and in our measurements it produced no stream change at all. Values from 100 to 500 gave identical output on five test signals at ELD plus SBR. The reason is most likely that the surrounding conditions are rarely all satisfied on this material, so the dominance test is not what decides. Treat it as available for experiments, not as a tool with a predictable result, and reach for --sbr-tran-thr instead.

Examples, lowering (consecutive slots count more readily):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-dom 130. Slightly below stock.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-dom 110. Nearly no dominance requirement.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-dom 100. The factor is 1.0, so a slot merely needs to match the two before it. No stream change observed.

Examples, raising (only decisive escalation counts):

  • Mild: --sbr-tran-dom 180. Above stock.
  • Strong: --sbr-tran-dom 300. A slot must be three times the level of its predecessors.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-tran-dom 500, the maximum. No stream change observed at any of these.

Works with / against: same ELD plus SBR fast detector as --sbr-tran-quiet, and the same limitation: nothing in HE-AAC. If you want the transient behaviour in that detector to change audibly, --sbr-tran-quiet in the 30..99 range is the one with measured effect.

Missing harmonics detector: --sbr-mh-tone --sbr-mh-diff --sbr-mh-decay-orig --sbr-mh-decay-diff --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr --sbr-mh-sfm-orig --sbr-mh-maxcomp --sbr-mh-deltatime

The transient detector deals with time. This detector deals with a problem created by the copying itself.

SBR builds the high band by patching lower spectrum upward. A tone that existed in the original high band is not guaranteed to survive that: the patch brings up whatever happened to be in the source region, so a real partial can land at the wrong frequency, arrive accompanied by partials that were never there, or simply vanish. On a glockenspiel, a triangle, a bell or a bright string harmonic this is exactly the content you notice. The missing harmonics detector compares the tonality of the original band with the tonality of the patched band, decides that a tone is missing, and signals the decoder to insert a synthetic sine in that band.

Both failure directions are audible. Too eager and you get whistling: steady, pitched tones up top that were never in the recording, often slightly off from the real harmonic series, which is the classic "SBR warble". Too conservative and bells and cymbals go dull as their partials quietly disappear.

Eight knobs reach into this detector. Be aware of their real status before spending time here: on our test material only two of them moved the stream at all. --sbr-mh-tone changed the output on a tonal signal at HE-AAC 24 kbps at values 1, 5 and 10, and --sbr-mh-diff at value 10. The other six produced no stream change at HE-AAC 24 to 96 kbps on tonal and mixed material, in either direction. That is a property of this detector on this material, not of the scaling: the x100 knobs here work in both directions and 100 is exactly stock. The detector simply needs several conditions to coincide before any of them decides the outcome, and on ordinary music it rarely gets there. Each card below says plainly where it stands.

--sbr-mh-tone <n>

Scales how tonal a band must be before SBR fabricates a synthetic harmonic in it, and it is the main control in this section.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

The detector measures tonality in the original band and compares it against a threshold. Scaling that threshold down makes more bands qualify as tonal, so more synthetic harmonics get added. Scaling it up makes fewer qualify.

What you hear in each direction is specific. More added tones means more of a bell's or cymbal's pitched content is restored, and more risk of whistling artefacts that were never in the source. Fewer added tones means a safer, duller top, with bright percussion losing its ring.

Measured: this is one of the two knobs in this section that moved the stream, on a tonal test signal at HE-AAC 24 kbps, at values 1, 5 and 10. Those are all far below stock, which tells you how low the threshold has to go before the detector changes its mind on that material.

Examples, lowering the threshold (more synthetic harmonics):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-tone 70. A modest widening of what counts as tonal. On much material this will not change the stream at all.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-tone 10. Measured to change the stream on tonal material. Expect more added tones and a brighter, more pitched high band.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-tone 1. Almost any band counts as tonal. Measured to change the stream, and the direction to use if you want to hear what whistling artefacts sound like.

Examples, raising the threshold (fewer synthetic harmonics):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-tone 150. Slightly stricter.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-tone 400. Only strongly tonal bands get a harmonic.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-tone 10000. The detector effectively never adds a harmonic. No stream change was observed above 100 on our material, so if you want this result, verify it on yours before relying on it.

Works with / against: --sbr-mh-diff is the other trigger path into the same decision, and --sbr-mh-sfm-orig / --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr move the tone versus noise line that feeds the tonality measurement. --sbr-mh-maxcomp controls what happens around a harmonic once it is added, not whether it is added.

--sbr-mh-diff <n>

Scales the original versus patched tonality difference that triggers harmonic compensation.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

--sbr-mh-tone asks "is the original band tonal enough". This asks a different question: "did the patching change the tonality". It is the path that catches the case where the original had one clean tone and the patched band has several, or the reverse, which is the specific damage copying does.

Lowering the threshold makes a smaller difference count as damage, so compensation triggers more often. Raising it means only a large mismatch counts.

Measured: this is the second of the two knobs in this section that moved the stream, at value 10 on our material. Values nearer stock, and values above stock, produced no change.

Examples, lowering (compensate on smaller mismatches):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-diff 70. Modest; may well change nothing on your material.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-diff 20. Close to the value where we saw a change.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-diff 10. Measured to change the stream. Almost any tonality mismatch between original and patch triggers compensation.

Examples, raising (compensate only on large mismatches):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-diff 150.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-diff 400.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-diff 10000. The difference path effectively never fires, leaving --sbr-mh-tone as the only trigger. No stream change observed upward on our material.

Works with / against: the two trigger paths, this one and --sbr-mh-tone, feed the same decision, so raising one while lowering the other partly cancels. The flatness knobs --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr and --sbr-mh-sfm-orig decide what counts as tonal in the first place on each side of the comparison.

--sbr-mh-decay-orig <n>

Scales how long an already detected tone keeps being tracked in the original band as it decays.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

A detector that only looked at the current frame would drop a tone as soon as it faded below threshold, which on a bell or cymbal tail is exactly when you still want it. So the detector keeps tracking a tone it has already found, with a decay. This scales the length of that memory.

Longer tracking means synthetic harmonics persist into tails, so decays stay pitched instead of turning into noise. Shorter tracking means tones are dropped sooner, so tails lose their ring earlier.

Honest status: wired, no effect observed. On tonal and mixed material at HE-AAC 24 to 96 kbps this produced no stream change in either direction. The decay only matters once the detector has found a tone to track, and the trigger conditions rarely coincided on our material.

Examples, shortening the tracking:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-decay-orig 70.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-decay-orig 30.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-decay-orig 1. Effectively no memory: a tone is forgotten as soon as it drops. No stream change observed.

Examples, lengthening the tracking:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-decay-orig 150.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-decay-orig 400.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-decay-orig 10000. A tone, once found, is tracked almost indefinitely. No stream change observed.

Works with / against: only relevant where the detector fires, so lowering --sbr-mh-tone into its measured active range is the prerequisite for this knob mattering at all. --sbr-mh-decay-diff is the same mechanism on the difference guide.

--sbr-mh-decay-diff <n>

The same decay as --sbr-mh-decay-orig, applied to the difference guide instead of the original band.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

The detector keeps two tracking guides: one following tones in the original band, one following the original versus patch tonality difference. This scales the memory of the second, so it governs how long a detected mismatch keeps being treated as present after it stops being obvious.

Longer means compensation decisions persist across frames and are more stable. Shorter means they are re-evaluated nearly from scratch each frame, which reacts faster and can flicker.

Honest status: wired, no effect observed. No stream change on tonal or mixed material at HE-AAC 24 to 96 kbps, in either direction.

Examples, shortening:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-decay-diff 70.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-decay-diff 30.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-decay-diff 1. No memory on the difference guide. No stream change observed.

Examples, lengthening:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-decay-diff 150.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-decay-diff 400.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-decay-diff 10000. The guide never forgets. No stream change observed.

Works with / against: pairs with --sbr-mh-decay-orig, and like it depends on --sbr-mh-diff actually triggering before the memory length can matter.

--sbr-mh-sfm-sbr <n>

Scales the spectral flatness threshold above which the patched band is judged noise like rather than tonal.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

Spectral flatness measures how evenly energy is spread across a band: near 0 for a single clean tone, near 1 for noise. The detector uses it to classify each band, and this knob moves the line for the patched side of the comparison.

Lowering the threshold means the patched band is called noise like sooner, so the detector more often concludes that the copy lost the tone and compensation is needed. Raising it means the patch is given the benefit of the doubt as tonal, so fewer harmonics are added.

Honest status: wired, no effect observed. No stream change on tonal or mixed material at HE-AAC 24 to 96 kbps in either direction.

One practical note if you measure this yourself: flatness has to be measured in the real SBR region, from the core bandwidth up to the final top edge as printed by --verbose. A window that runs to Nyquist includes the empty part above the top edge and returns a flatness of 0, which looks like perfect tonality and is an artefact of the window.

Examples, lowering (patch called noise like sooner, more compensation):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr 70.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr 30.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr 1. Nearly any patched band counts as noise like. No stream change observed.

Examples, raising (patch called tonal for longer, less compensation):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr 150.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr 400.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr 10000. The patched band is effectively always tonal. No stream change observed.

Works with / against: works as a pair with --sbr-mh-sfm-orig; between them they catch "one tone in the original became several after patching". Moving only one side shifts the comparison asymmetrically, which is sometimes what you want and is harder to reason about.

--sbr-mh-sfm-orig <n>

The same flatness threshold as --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr, for the original band.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1..10000, x100 where 100 = stock x1.00; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

This moves the tone versus noise line on the source side. Lowering it means the original band is called noise like sooner, so the detector concludes less often that there was a tone worth restoring. Raising it means the original is treated as tonal more readily, so more bands become candidates for a synthetic harmonic.

Note that the two sfm knobs push the final decision in opposite senses despite being the same kind of threshold, because one describes what you had and the other what you got. If you want "add more harmonics", lower --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr and raise this one.

Honest status: wired, no effect observed. No stream change on tonal or mixed material at HE-AAC 24 to 96 kbps in either direction.

Examples, lowering (original called noise like sooner, fewer harmonics):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-sfm-orig 70.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-sfm-orig 30.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-sfm-orig 1. The source is almost never tonal, so almost nothing is judged missing. No stream change observed.

Examples, raising (original called tonal more readily, more harmonics):

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-sfm-orig 150.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-sfm-orig 400.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-sfm-orig 10000. Nearly every original band counts as tonal. No stream change observed.

Works with / against: the mirror of --sbr-mh-sfm-sbr, and both feed the tonality comparison that --sbr-mh-diff thresholds. Changing all three at once makes the result very hard to attribute; move one at a time.

--sbr-mh-maxcomp <n>

Caps how much envelope compensation is applied around a synthetic harmonic.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0..200, a plain integer where 50 = stock; default -1 = off
  • Input: whole number

Inserting a sine into a band changes the energy of that band, so the envelope around it has to be adjusted or the band ends up too loud. This caps that adjustment. It is not an x100 scale: the number is the limit itself.

A higher cap allows larger corrections, so neighbouring bands are pulled further and less noise is left sitting next to the added tone: the harmonic stands out more cleanly. A lower cap limits the correction, so the added tone sits in more of its original surrounding noise and blends in, or becomes audibly too loud if the correction needed was larger than the cap.

Honest status: wired, no effect observed. No stream change on tonal or mixed material at HE-AAC 24 to 96 kbps in either direction, which follows from the detector rarely adding a harmonic on this material in the first place.

Examples, lowering the cap:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-maxcomp 35.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-maxcomp 10. Very little compensation allowed.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-maxcomp 0. No compensation at all around an added harmonic. No stream change observed.

Examples, raising the cap:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-maxcomp 70.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-maxcomp 120.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-maxcomp 200, the maximum. Correction is effectively unlimited. No stream change observed.

Works with / against: only applies where a harmonic is actually added, so --sbr-mh-tone has to be in its active range first. The noise that sits next to the added tone is also governed by --sbr-noise-max and the noise floor knobs, which act on every band rather than just the harmonic's neighbours.

--sbr-mh-deltatime <n>

Sets the maximum transient distance for a frame to count as a transient frame inside the missing harmonics detector.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0..64, a plain integer; default -1 = off, which leaves 9 for AAC and 16 for AAC-LD
  • Input: whole number

The missing harmonics detector behaves differently in frames it considers transient, because adding a steady synthetic sine across an attack is a bad idea. This sets how far, in detector time units, a transient may be from the frame for the frame still to count as transient. It is a plain integer, not an x100 scale, and the stock value differs by profile.

A larger distance means more frames count as transient, so the detector takes its cautious path more often and adds fewer harmonics in the neighbourhood of hits. A smaller distance means fewer frames count as transient, so the detector adds harmonics closer to attacks, which is where a synthetic sine is most likely to sound wrong.

Honest status: wired, no effect observed. No stream change on tonal or mixed material at HE-AAC 24 to 96 kbps in either direction.

Examples, lowering the distance:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-deltatime 6.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-deltatime 2.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-deltatime 0. No frame counts as transient on distance, so the cautious path is never taken. No stream change observed.

Examples, raising the distance:

  • Mild: --sbr-mh-deltatime 16, which is the AAC-LD stock value applied to AAC.
  • Strong: --sbr-mh-deltatime 32.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-mh-deltatime 64, the maximum. Almost every frame near any hit counts as transient. No stream change observed.

Works with / against: this reads the transient decision, so it is downstream of --sbr-tran-thr and --sbr-tran-peak: changing what counts as a transient changes what this knob is measuring distance to. It has no influence on framing itself.

Inverse filtering and noise floor: --sbr-invf --sbr-softness --sbr-invf-min --sbr-invf-max --sbr-invf-bias --sbr-invf-table --sbr-invf-hyst --sbr-invf-sbr-thr --sbr-invf-orig-thr --sbr-invf-smooth --sbr-nf-weight --sbr-nf-weight-from --sbr-nf-smooth --sbr-noise-max --sbr-noise-floor-offset

This is where the character of the high band is actually decided, and it is the part of SBR worth understanding properly.

The copied high band inherits the fine structure of the band it was copied from. That structure is tonal: peaks and valleys at the wrong frequencies for where it has landed. The result sounds metallic, ringing, sometimes chorused, and once you have heard it you cannot unhear it. Inverse filtering is the cure: the decoder applies a whitening filter to the patch, flattening that inherited structure. There are four strengths, 0 off, 1 low, 2 mid, 3 high, chosen per band and per frame. The noise floor is the other half of the same cure: synthetic noise mixed into each band to replace the structure that whitening removed. Too little of both and the top is metallic; too much and it is hissy and washed out, with cymbals reduced to shaped noise.

The level is normally chosen by a tonality estimator that compares the original band with the patched band, band by band and frame by frame. That dynamic decision is the thing to preserve: it is right much more often than any fixed value you could pick.

So this section has two kinds of switch, and the priority between them matters:

  1. --sbr-invf forces one fixed level for the whole file, in every band and every moment. It wins over everything else here. Use it to diagnose, or when you know the material is uniform.
  2. Everything else leaves the decision dynamic and changes the estimator's rules: bounds, bias, decision table, hysteresis, smoothing, border positions, noise weight. The decision is still made per band and per frame, just along different rules.

Within group 2, --sbr-softness is a macro that sets several of those rules at once, and every explicit knob wins over the macro. So --sbr-softness 6 --sbr-invf-max 2 gives you the macro's softness with your own ceiling.

Two measurement facts shape how you should use this section.

First, the softness macro deliberately does not move the tonality borders. Shifting them was measured to make the per band decision flicker, 0.2 to 0.29 changes per band per frame against 0.004 to 0.04 for stock, which is audible as instability. --sbr-invf-sbr-thr and --sbr-invf-orig-thr expose that mechanism anyway, because sometimes you want it, but that is why the macro avoids them.

Second, the noise floor weight is clipped by the noise ceiling. With the tuning table's ceiling (0.125 at 24 to 48 kbps) about 95 percent of bands already sit at the ceiling, so --sbr-nf-weight on its own frequently changes nothing at all. It needs a raised ceiling and high invf bands to do anything: measured, it moved the stream only with --sbr-noise-max 6 plus either --sbr-invf 3 or --sbr-nf-weight-from 1. Alone, or with only one of those, nothing changed. Levels 5 to 7 of --sbr-softness raise the ceiling for you.

At their neutral values (--sbr-invf-hyst 1.0, --sbr-invf-bias 0, --sbr-invf-smooth 2, --sbr-nf-weight 0.25, --sbr-nf-weight-from 3, --sbr-invf-table 0, --sbr-invf-min 0, --sbr-invf-max 3) these knobs give a stream byte identical to stock, as do --sbr-softness 2 and --speech --sbr-softness 4.

--sbr-invf <0..3>

Forces one fixed inverse filtering level for the whole file, overriding the tonality estimator completely.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles (-p 5, -p 29, -p 39 -L 1)
  • Values: 0 off, 1 low, 2 mid, 3 high; default -1 = auto, the tonality estimator decides per band and per frame
  • Input: whole number
