Higher means more whitening of the patched band: less metallic ringing, less detail, a flatter and more noise like top. Lower means the inherited tonal structure is left in place: more apparent detail, more metallic character.

The drawback of forcing is exactly what it says: one level applies everywhere, including in the bands and moments where the estimator would have chosen differently. A file with a quiet tonal intro and a dense chorus wants different levels in those places, and a forced value gives them the same one.

That said, this is the most useful knob here for two jobs. The first is diagnosis: encode at 0 and at 3 and listen to the extremes, and you will immediately know which end of the axis your material needs. The second is uniform material, where there is nothing for the estimator to adapt to anyway.

Values:

  • 0 inverse filtering off. No whitening at all. The patch keeps its inherited structure: maximum apparent detail, maximum metallic ringing. On tonal material this is clearly an artefact; on noise like material it can be the cleanest option.
  • 1 low. A light whitening.
  • 2 mid. Moderate whitening, which is roughly where the estimator sits on average with the music table.
  • 3 high. Strong whitening. The top becomes flat and noise like; metallic ringing disappears and so does a good deal of perceived detail.
  • -1 auto. The estimator decides. The recommended setting unless you are diagnosing.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-invf 3 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: this wins over every other switch in this section, including --sbr-softness and the min/max/bias knobs, because there is no dynamic decision left to bound. If you want to shape the decision rather than replace it, leave this off. Pairing --sbr-invf 3 with --sbr-noise-max 6 is one of the two combinations that makes --sbr-nf-weight do anything.

--sbr-softness <0..7>

One macro from a hard, metallic top to a very soft one, setting several estimator rules at once while keeping the decision dynamic.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0..7; default -1 = off (stock, which is equivalent to level 2 for music)
  • Input: whole number

This is the knob to try first. It moves you along the metallic versus hissy axis without freezing the decision, by setting the decision table, the bias, the noise weight, the min/max bounds and the noise ceiling in combinations that were measured to be monotonic and stable. It deliberately does not touch the tonality borders, because doing that was measured to make the per band decision flicker.

Values:

  • 0 one step less whitening than the music tuning: harder, more metallic than stock.
  • 1 never HIGH whitening. Note that on music the estimator rarely picks HIGH anyway, so on music material this bound often never binds: measured at HE-AAC 32 kbps, levels 1 and 2 gave a stream identical to stock on tonal, voice and mixed signals.
  • 2 stock music estimators. Byte identical to not using the switch at all.
  • 3 at least LOW whitening: the estimator may no longer choose off. Measured equal to --sbr-invf 1 on a tonal signal, because there the estimator wanted off everywhere and the floor lifted it all to LOW.
  • 4 stock speech estimators: the speech decision table and noise weight, without the rest of --speech, so the crossover is untouched.
  • 5 as 4 plus a raised noise ceiling (0.5) and the weight extended to MID bands.
  • 6 as 5 plus one more step of whitening.
  • 7 very soft: ceiling 1.0, maximum whitening and fill noise. The top becomes unambiguously noise like.
  • -1 off.

Measured at HE-AAC 32 kbps, invf levels read back from the bitstream, flatness of the decoded SBR band 7 to 14.5 kHz where 0 is tonal and 1 is noise like:

--sbr-softness mean invf level (speech / tonal / music) SBR band flatness (speech / tonal)
0 0.15 / 0.00 / 0.00 0.366 / 0.396
2 (stock music) 1.12 / 0.98 / 0.99 0.373 / 0.430
4 (stock speech) 1.93 / 1.96 / 1.98 0.379 / 0.440
5 1.93 / 1.96 / 1.98 0.403 / 0.518
7 2.93 / 2.96 / 2.98 0.417 / 0.521

The scale is monotonic on every sample, and per band mode changes stay at the stock rate (0.004 to 0.04 per band per frame). Also measured: level 0 equals --sbr-invf 0 on tonal and mixed material, and levels 4 through 7 are distinct from each other.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 32000 --sbr-softness 6 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: every explicit knob below wins over this macro, which is how you combine them: --sbr-softness 6 --sbr-invf-max 2 keeps the macro's noise handling with your own ceiling. --sbr-invf wins over this entirely. Neighbouring levels can give the same stream when your material never reaches the bound they differ in; that is expected, not a failure.

--sbr-invf-min <0..3>

Sets a floor under the estimator: the lowest inverse filtering level it is allowed to pick.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0..3; default -1 = off (no floor, equivalent to 0)
  • Input: whole number

The estimator keeps deciding per band and per frame; this just removes the bottom of its range. It is the tool for "the top is metallic in the quiet passages", where the estimator correctly sees a tonal band and chooses little or no whitening, and you disagree.

Raising the floor guarantees a minimum amount of whitening everywhere, which costs detail in the bands that genuinely did not need it.

Values:

  • 0 floor at off. No constraint; identical to not using the switch.
  • 1 the estimator may never choose off. The gentlest useful floor, and the one worth trying when metallic ringing appears only occasionally.
  • 2 at least MID whitening everywhere. A strong intervention: bands that wanted none now get moderate whitening.
  • 3 at least HIGH everywhere, which means the level is pinned to HIGH since 3 is the maximum. That makes it equivalent in effect to --sbr-invf 3, and if that is what you want, say so directly.
  • -1 off.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 48000 --sbr-invf-min 1 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: applied after --sbr-invf-bias, together with --sbr-invf-max as the other end of the clamp. Setting --sbr-invf-min 3 or --sbr-invf-min equal to --sbr-invf-max collapses the dynamic range to a single value, which is --sbr-invf the long way round. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf. --sbr-softness 3 is the packaged version of --sbr-invf-min 1.

--sbr-invf-max <0..3>

Sets a ceiling over the estimator: the highest inverse filtering level it is allowed to pick.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0..3; default -1 = off (no ceiling, equivalent to 3)
  • Input: whole number

The mirror of --sbr-invf-min, and the more frequently useful of the two. It is the tool for "the top goes hissy and loses detail on the loud passages", where the estimator picks strong whitening and takes the sparkle with it.

Lowering the ceiling preserves detail at the price of letting some metallic character through in the bands that genuinely needed whitening.

Values:

  • 3 ceiling at HIGH. No constraint; identical to not using the switch.
  • 2 never HIGH. The usual choice: it removes only the most aggressive whitening and keeps the rest of the estimator's judgement.
  • 1 never above LOW. A strong detail preserving setting; expect audible metallic character on tonal material.
  • 0 the ceiling is off, so no whitening is permitted anywhere. Equivalent in effect to --sbr-invf 0, and better written that way.
  • -1 off.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-invf-max 2 --sbr-invf-hyst 1.5 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: applied after --sbr-invf-bias, paired with --sbr-invf-min. --sbr-softness 1 is the packaged version of --sbr-invf-max 2, and note the measured caveat there: on music the estimator rarely reaches HIGH, so this ceiling can be a no-op on music material and still be exactly right on speech. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf.

--sbr-invf-bias <-3..3>

Shifts every dynamic inverse filtering decision up or down by a fixed number of steps.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: -3..3 steps; default 0 = off
  • Input: whole number

Where min and max clip the range, this slides it. The estimator's per band, per frame pattern of decisions is preserved exactly, and the whole pattern moves. That makes it the cleanest way to say "the estimator has the right idea, it is just consistently too soft" or "consistently too hard". Positive means more whitening.

Because the result is clamped to 0..3 afterwards, large biases flatten the pattern at whichever end they run into: at +3 almost everything lands on HIGH and the per band variation you were trying to keep is gone.

Examples, more whitening:

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-bias 1. Every band one step more whitened. The pattern is intact, the character is softer.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-bias 2. Two steps. Bands that wanted off now get MID; metallic character is largely gone and so is some detail.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-bias 3. Everything the estimator did not already want at HIGH gets pushed there by the clamp. This is --sbr-invf 3 with extra steps.

Examples, less whitening:

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-bias -1. One step less everywhere. More detail, a hint more metallic.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-bias -2. Two steps less. Only the bands the estimator wanted at HIGH retain any whitening.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-bias -3. Nearly everything clamps to off, which is --sbr-invf 0 the long way round, with maximum metallic ringing on tonal material.

Works with / against: applied before --sbr-invf-min and --sbr-invf-max, so you can bias and then clamp: --sbr-invf-bias 2 --sbr-invf-max 2 shifts everything up but refuses HIGH. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf.

--sbr-invf-table <0|1>

Chooses which decision table the tonality estimator uses: the music table or the speech table.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0 music, 1 speech; default -1 = follows --speech
  • Input: whole number

The estimator's thresholds come from a table, and FDK ships two: one tuned for music, one for speech. The speech table whitens considerably more readily, because speech above the crossover is mostly fricatives and breath, where inherited tonal structure is pure artefact and there is no detail worth protecting.

This switch picks the table and only the table. --speech also changes the crossover and other tuning; if you want the speech estimator behaviour on music material without rearranging the frequency layout, this is the switch for it. That combination is genuinely useful: spoken word programme, audiobooks, podcast material at low bitrate, where you want the whitening behaviour but your own crossover.

Values:

  • 0 music table. Conservative whitening, protects tonal detail.
  • 1 speech table. Whitens much more readily. Measured mean invf level with the speech table is near 2.0 against about 1.0 for the music table at HE-AAC 32 kbps.
  • -1 off: follow --speech, so music table unless --speech is given.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 32000 --sbr-invf-table 1 -o out.m4a speech.wav

Works with / against: --sbr-softness 4 is the packaged version of this plus the speech noise weight. --speech sets this implicitly and also moves the crossover, which is the difference worth remembering. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf.

--sbr-invf-hyst <x>

Sets the hysteresis of the decision borders: how much the tonality has to change before the estimator switches a band to a different level.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0..2, decimal where 1.0 = stock; default -1 = off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

A band whose tonality sits right on a decision border would otherwise flip level every frame, and flipping whitening levels frame to frame is audible as a kind of fluttering or unsteadiness up top. Hysteresis widens the borders into a dead zone, so a band keeps its current level until the tonality has moved meaningfully past the line.

Lowering it reacts faster to genuine changes and switches more often. Raising it is more stable and slower to follow real changes, so a passage that turns tonal part way through keeps the previous treatment for longer. Above 2 the widened borders would overlap each other, which has no meaning, so that is rejected.

For reference, the stock per band switching rate is 0.004 to 0.04 changes per band per frame, and a configuration measured at 0.2 to 0.29 was clearly unstable.

Examples, less hysteresis (faster, more switching):

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-hyst 0.8. Slightly quicker to follow changes.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-hyst 0.3. Clearly more switching; use when the estimator feels sluggish on material that changes character quickly.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-hyst 0. No dead zone at all. Bands sitting on a border flip every frame, which is the instability hysteresis exists to prevent.

Examples, more hysteresis (slower, more stable):

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-hyst 1.2. A little stickier than stock.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-hyst 1.5. Levels hold through moderate tonality changes. A good pairing with --sbr-invf-max 2 when you want a calm, predictable top.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-hyst 2, the maximum. The dead zones are as wide as they can be; bands essentially keep whatever level they first landed on, which on material that changes character is simply the wrong level most of the time.

Works with / against: --sbr-invf-smooth attacks the same instability from the other side, by averaging over frames instead of widening borders; using both at their extremes is redundant. --sbr-invf-sbr-thr and --sbr-invf-orig-thr move the borders this knob is widening. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf.

--sbr-invf-sbr-thr <x>

Shifts the tonality decision borders for the patched band, roughly in dB.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: -20..20, about dB, decimal; default 0 = off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

The estimator measures tonality on both sides of the comparison and compares each against borders. This moves the borders on the patched side. Positive means the patched band counts as tonal sooner, and a tonal patch is what whitening is for, so positive means more invf.

This is a sharper instrument than bias or min/max, and a riskier one. Moving borders changes which bands cross them and in which frames, so it can make the decision flicker: this mechanism measured 0.2 to 0.29 changes per band per frame against 0.004 to 0.04 for stock, which is why --sbr-softness does not use it. If you reach for this, consider raising --sbr-invf-hyst at the same time to buy the stability back.

Examples, positive (patched band tonal sooner, more invf):

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-sbr-thr 2. A small shift; a few more bands cross into whitening.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-sbr-thr 8. Many more bands count as tonal. Expect noticeably more whitening and some flicker.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-sbr-thr 20, the maximum. Essentially every patched band counts as tonal, so the measurement no longer discriminates and the estimator's judgement is replaced by a constant. --sbr-invf 3 does the same thing without the flicker.

Examples, negative (patched band tonal later, less invf):

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-sbr-thr -2. Slightly fewer bands whitened.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-sbr-thr -8. Only strongly tonal patches get whitened; more detail, more metallic character.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-sbr-thr -20, the minimum. Nothing counts as tonal, so nothing is whitened. The caricature of "preserve all detail", and it sounds like it.

Works with / against: the mirror of --sbr-invf-orig-thr, and the two combine into the original versus patch comparison. --sbr-invf-hyst is the stability counterweight. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf.

--sbr-invf-orig-thr <x>

Shifts the tonality decision borders for the original band, roughly in dB.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: -20..20, about dB, decimal; default 0 = off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

The same mechanism on the source side, and the sign reads the other way round, which is the thing to get right. The estimator whitens when the patch looks more tonal than the original deserves. So making the original count as tonal later means the comparison finds a bigger mismatch more often, and positive values therefore also mean more whitening. Both threshold knobs point the same way in outcome despite describing opposite sides.

The flicker caveat applies here identically: this is a border shift, and border shifts destabilise the per band decision more than bias or clamping do.

Examples, positive (original counts as tonal later, more invf):

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-orig-thr 2.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-orig-thr 8. The original is credited with tonality rarely, so the patch looks wrong more often and gets whitened.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-orig-thr 20, the maximum. The original never counts as tonal, so every patch is treated as damage. Maximum whitening by a route that also maximises flicker.

Examples, negative (original counts as tonal sooner, less invf):

  • Mild: --sbr-invf-orig-thr -2.
  • Strong: --sbr-invf-orig-thr -8. The original is generously credited as tonal, so the patch is more often judged acceptable and left alone.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-invf-orig-thr -20, the minimum. Everything in the original counts as tonal, the comparison finds no mismatch, and whitening effectively stops.

Works with / against: pairs with --sbr-invf-sbr-thr; moving both in the same direction partly cancels, since one raises the mismatch and the other lowers it. The noise floor reads the same original versus patch gap through --sbr-nf-weight, so shifting these borders changes the fill noise as well, not just the whitening. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf.

--sbr-invf-smooth <0..2>

Sets how many frames the tonality estimator averages before deciding.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0, 1, 2; default -1 = off (stock averages 2)
  • Input: whole number

Averaging over frames is the other defence against an unstable decision. With averaging, one anomalous frame cannot flip a band's whitening level; without it, each frame is judged on its own. The trade is the usual one: stability against reaction speed.

Values:

  • 0 this frame only. Fastest reaction to a genuine change of character, most frame to frame switching. Use on material that changes quickly, and expect some unsteadiness.
  • 1 a shorter average than stock. The middle option.
  • 2 stock. The balance FDK chose, and a good one on most material.
  • -1 off, which is the same as 2.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-invf-smooth 0 --sbr-invf-hyst 1.5 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: this and --sbr-invf-hyst both buy stability, by different means, so pairing low smoothing with high hysteresis is a reasonable way to get fast reaction without flutter. Wins over --sbr-softness; loses to --sbr-invf, which has no decision left to smooth.

--sbr-nf-weight <x>

Sets how strongly the original versus patch tonality gap raises the noise floor.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles, and only where the noise ceiling leaves room (see below)
  • Values: 0.0..1.0, decimal; default -1 = follows --speech (music 0.25, speech 1.0)
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

Whitening removes structure; fill noise replaces it. This weight decides how much of the measured mismatch between the original and the patched band is converted into noise floor. Higher means more fill noise wherever the patch is too tonal, which is why the speech tuning uses 1.0: in speech the high band should be noise, so filling it is always right. Lower means the gap is mostly handled by whitening alone.

Read this before using it. The weighted noise is clipped by the noise ceiling, and with the tuning table's ceiling (0.125 at 24 to 48 kbps) about 95 percent of bands are already at the ceiling, so raising the weight changes nothing because there is no headroom to use. Measured, this knob moved the stream only with --sbr-noise-max 6 plus either --sbr-invf 3 or --sbr-nf-weight-from 1. Alone, or with only one of those two, nothing changed at all. It needs a raised ceiling and bands at a high invf level, since by default only HIGH bands receive the weight.

So the working recipe is --sbr-noise-max 6 --sbr-nf-weight-from 1 --sbr-nf-weight <x>, or let --sbr-softness 5, 6 or 7 raise the ceiling for you.

Examples, more fill noise (with the ceiling raised):

  • Mild: --sbr-noise-max 6 --sbr-nf-weight-from 1 --sbr-nf-weight 0.4. A little more air where the patch is too tonal.
  • Strong: --sbr-noise-max 6 --sbr-nf-weight-from 1 --sbr-nf-weight 0.7. Clearly more fill noise; the top reads as airier and less metallic, with less apparent detail.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-noise-max 6 --sbr-nf-weight-from 1 --sbr-nf-weight 1, the maximum, which is the speech value applied to every band from LOW upward with the ceiling wide open. On music this is hiss where cymbals used to be.

Examples, less fill noise:

  • Mild: --sbr-nf-weight 0.15. Below the music default; slightly drier top.
  • Strong: --sbr-nf-weight 0.05. The tonality gap contributes almost no noise; whitening has to do the work alone.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-nf-weight 0, the minimum. The gap is ignored entirely. With the default ceiling this may well be bit identical to stock, since most bands are clipped anyway, which is itself the lesson about this knob.

Works with / against: needs --sbr-noise-max 3 or 6 to have headroom, and --sbr-nf-weight-from to reach bands below HIGH. --sbr-noise-floor-offset raises the floor unconditionally and is the blunter alternative when this one refuses to move. Wins over --sbr-softness; --sbr-invf 3 does not block it, it is one of the two things that enables it.

--sbr-nf-weight-from <1..3>

Sets the lowest inverse filtering level whose bands receive the noise floor weight.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 1 LOW and above, 2 MID and above, 3 HIGH only; default -1 = off (stock is 3)
  • Input: whole number

The tonality gap weight is not applied everywhere. By default only bands that got HIGH whitening receive it, on the reasoning that those are the bands where structure was really destroyed and needs replacing. This switch widens that set downward.

That makes it the enabling switch for --sbr-nf-weight: on music the estimator rarely picks HIGH, so with the stock setting the weight has almost no bands to act on, and the knob looks dead. Set this to 1 and every whitened band is in scope.

Values:

  • 3 HIGH only. Stock. The most conservative, and the reason --sbr-nf-weight so often appears to do nothing on music.
  • 2 MID and HIGH. A moderate widening, and what --sbr-softness 5 and above do.
  • 1 LOW, MID and HIGH. Every band with any whitening gets the weight. The widest scope, and the one measured to let --sbr-nf-weight change the stream when paired with --sbr-noise-max 6.
  • -1 off, same as 3.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 32000 --sbr-noise-max 6 --sbr-nf-weight-from 1 --sbr-nf-weight 0.7 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: pointless without --sbr-nf-weight doing something, and --sbr-nf-weight is pointless without headroom from --sbr-noise-max. All three move together. Wins over --sbr-softness.

--sbr-nf-smooth <0>

Turns off the time smoothing of the noise floor.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0 = no smoothing; default -1 = off, which leaves the stock smoothing over 4 frames
  • Input: whole number

The noise floor is normally averaged over four frames, so the amount of fill noise in a band changes gradually. That is right on steady material, where a jumping noise level would be audible as a pumping or breathing quality in the top end. It is wrong where the material genuinely changes fast: the smoothed floor arrives late, so a sudden quiet passage still carries the noise of the loud one for a few frames, and a sudden bright passage is under filled at its start.

This switch is one directional. There is no value that increases the smoothing; 0 removes it and anything else means leave it alone.

Values:

  • 0 no time smoothing. The noise floor follows the frame by frame estimate exactly. Faster, and on steady material audibly less stable.
  • -1 off: stock smoothing over 4 frames. The right default.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 64000 --sbr-nf-smooth 0 -o out.m4a transients.wav

Works with / against: the noise analogue of --sbr-invf-smooth 0, and the same reasoning applies: use it on material with fast changes of character, and expect less stability on sustained material. Its effect is bounded by --sbr-noise-max: if the ceiling is clipping most bands, there is little variation left for smoothing to smooth. Wins over --sbr-softness.

--sbr-noise-max <6|3|-3>

Sets the ceiling on how loud the noise SBR injects may get: the air versus hiss limit up top.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 6 = 1.0, 3 = 0.5, -3 = 0.125; default -1 = the value from the bitrate tuning table
  • Input: whole number

This is the most consequential noise knob, not because it sets the level but because it sets the limit everything else runs into. The three values are the three the FDK noise estimator understands, and they correspond to amplitude ceilings of 1.0, 0.5 and 0.125. The tuning table picks 0.125 at 24 to 48 kbps, and at that ceiling about 95 percent of bands are already clipped, which is why fill noise feels fixed until you raise it.

Raising the ceiling does not by itself add noise; it allows the estimator and the weight to add more where they already wanted to. Lowering it caps the top end into something drier and more tonal, which on sparse material is cleaner and on cymbals and breath sounds thin.

Which of the three values is a no-op depends on the tuning table row you are on, so measure rather than assume. Measured: on tonal and mixed signals at HE-AAC 32 kbps, -3 equals stock and 6 equals 3; on a voice signal at the same setting, 6, 3 and stock are all the same.

Values:

  • -3 ceiling 0.125. The driest, and the table's choice at low bitrate. More tonal, more metallic, less hiss.
  • 3 ceiling 0.5. Four times the headroom. Fill noise can actually vary band to band.
  • 6 ceiling 1.0. Maximum headroom. Paired with --sbr-nf-weight and --sbr-nf-weight-from 1 this is the configuration where the noise floor knobs finally have room to work; on its own it mostly lifts the bands the estimator was already pushing against the ceiling.
  • -1 off: the bitrate tuning table decides.

Example in context: fdkaac -p 5 -b 32000 --sbr-noise-max 6 --sbr-nf-weight-from 1 --sbr-nf-weight 0.7 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: this is the gate in front of --sbr-nf-weight, --sbr-nf-weight-from and --sbr-nf-smooth, and --sbr-softness levels 5 to 7 raise it for you (5 and 6 to 0.5, 7 to 1.0). --sbr-noise-bands sets the frequency resolution of the floor this ceiling caps, so a dense floor at a low ceiling is wasted side information.

--sbr-noise-floor-offset <n>

Offsets the whole SBR noise floor, adding or removing fill noise everywhere at once.

  • Applies to: SBR profiles
  • Values: 0..11; default -128 = off
  • Input: whole number

Where --sbr-nf-weight adds noise conditionally, based on a measured mismatch, this simply shifts the floor. Bigger means more fill noise in the reconstruction across the board, which reads as more air and more hiss, and smothers whatever inherited tonal structure survived whitening. Smaller means a drier, more tonal top.

That bluntness is sometimes exactly what you want. When --sbr-nf-weight refuses to move because every band is clipped at the ceiling, this is the knob that still works, because it acts on the floor itself rather than on the gap measurement.

The accepted range is 0..11 and nothing else. Outside it, FDK's own noise handling hits an assertion and aborts, so the encoder rejects out of range values with a clean error rather than letting that happen.

Examples, more fill noise:

  • Mild: --sbr-noise-floor-offset 3. A touch more air up top.
  • Strong: --sbr-noise-floor-offset 7. Clearly noisier top; metallic character is buried and so is fine detail.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-noise-floor-offset 11, the maximum. The high band is dominated by fill noise. Cymbals become shaped hiss, and the result is unambiguously a caricature, which makes it a useful reference point for how much of your top end is noise.

Examples, less fill noise:

  • Mild: --sbr-noise-floor-offset 2. Slightly drier.
  • Strong: --sbr-noise-floor-offset 1. Minimal fill; the patched band's own structure and whatever whitening leaves behind carry the top end.
  • Exaggerated: --sbr-noise-floor-offset 0, the minimum. No offset contribution at all. On tonal material expect the metallic ringing that fill noise exists to mask, which pairs badly with low --sbr-invf settings and is worth hearing once for exactly that reason.

Works with / against: still subject to --sbr-noise-max, so a high offset against the 0.125 ceiling clips. Works in the same direction as --sbr-nf-weight but unconditionally, which makes it the right choice when you want a global change and the wrong one when you want the encoder to decide where noise belongs. Against low --sbr-invf levels it is fighting a different artefact with the wrong tool: metallic ringing from un whitened structure is better fixed with whitening than masked with noise.

D. Masking, noise shaping and detail

This is the group that decides how much noise the encoder is willing to leave in each band, and where it fabricates signal instead of coding it. Four ideas carry the whole group, so they are explained once here and the cards refer back.

Masking threshold. For every scalefactor band, in every frame, the psychoacoustic model computes a level below which added noise should be inaudible, because louder content in and around that band covers it. That level is the masking threshold. The quantiser then spends only as many bits as it takes to push the quantisation noise down to the threshold, and not one more. Raise the thresholds globally and the encoder decides more noise is acceptable, so it spends fewer bits and the file gets coarser at the same bitrate; lower them and it demands more precision everywhere and runs out of bits sooner, which under CBR means something else has to give. --ath-scale is the global scale on this threshold. --spread-mask changes how far masking leaks between neighbouring bands, which is a different question: not how loud the mask is, but how wide.

SNR demand. Separately from the threshold, FDK keeps a required signal to noise ratio per band, the minimum quality the coder must deliver in that band whatever the threshold says. This is the closest FDK gets to the tone masking noise and noise masking tone constants that MusePack and LAME expose. It matters more than it sounds, because the hole avoidance logic clamps thresholds back to this demand: if the rate loop wants to starve a band, the SNR demand is the line it is not allowed to cross. --minsnr-scale scales the demand, and the two clamp switches move the ceiling and the floor the demand itself is held between.

TNS, temporal noise shaping. Inside one block, quantisation noise is spread evenly across the whole block in time. That is wrong for a transient: a block containing a drum hit has noise sitting in the quiet part before the hit, where nothing masks it, which you hear as pre-echo. TNS applies a prediction filter across frequency, which has the effect of shaping the noise envelope in time so it follows the signal envelope and hides under the attack. --tns-mask chooses which of the filters are allowed to run and --tns-order how long they may be. For LD and ELD this is the main defence against pre-echo, since those profiles have no short blocks at all.

PNS, perceptual noise substitution. When a band is just noise, coding its exact spectral lines is a waste: nobody can tell one noise realisation from another at that level. PNS throws the lines away and stores a single number, the band's energy, with a flag saying "noise here". The decoder regenerates random noise scaled to that energy. The saving on noisy material is large. The risk is that a band which is not really noise gets substituted, and then a genuine tone turns into hiss. Eight switches in this group control PNS: whether it runs at all, from which frequency, whether the low bitrate gate applies, how loud the fabricated noise is, and three detection thresholds plus a minimum band width that together decide which bands qualify.

One convention before the cards: all the --pns-* scaling knobs take a plain decimal multiplier on FDK's factory value. 1.0 means leave the factory value alone, 1.5 means one and a half times it, 0.5 means half. --pns-min-width is the exception, it is a raw count.

--ath-scale <n>

The global masking threshold scale: one knob for "be cleaner" or "be more economical" across the whole spectrum.

  • Applies to: every profile with an AAC core, so all of them. Under SBR it affects the core only, since SBR has its own noise model.
  • Values: Q8, 1..4096, where 256 is x1.0; default: 256, no change
  • Input: whole number

The masking threshold is the noise level the model believes you cannot hear in a given band. --ath-scale multiplies all of them at once. Above 256 the thresholds rise, the encoder concludes more noise is inaudible, and it spends fewer bits per band: more aggressive coding, which at a fixed bitrate means those bits go somewhere else or the frame simply finishes early. Below 256 the thresholds fall, more content is deemed worth coding accurately, and the demand for bits rises.

Two things keep this knob from being as strong as its range suggests. It operates on the log domain threshold copy, and part of its effect is undone downstream by the minimum SNR logic and the 29 dB threshold reduction ceiling in the CBR quantiser. And under CBR a global threshold change mostly reshuffles bits rather than changing their number, because the rate loop still has to hit the target. If you want a stronger and more direct lever on detail, --minsnr-scale is the one, because it moves the line that the hole avoidance clamps back to.

Where it earns its keep is at high bitrate, where you have surplus bits and want the encoder to find more things worth coding. Values a little below 256 convert that surplus into preserved detail. At low bitrate the useful direction is usually upward, as a way of accepting coarseness deliberately rather than having the rate loop decide where to cut.

Examples:

  • Mild, cleaner: --ath-scale 230. Thresholds about 10 percent lower. Slightly more bits to quiet detail; subtle at any bitrate, most visible above 192 kbps.
  • Strong, cleaner: --ath-scale 180. A clear shift of effort towards low level content. Reverb tails and room noise survive better; at a tight bitrate this starves something else.
  • Exaggerated, cleaner: --ath-scale 64. Thresholds a quarter of stock. The model is effectively told almost nothing is masked. Under CBR the rate loop fights back all frame long and the result is not better, just differently compromised; worth hearing once to understand where the limit sits.
  • Mild, coarser: --ath-scale 290. A little more noise accepted per band, a small bit saving.
  • Strong, coarser: --ath-scale 512. Thresholds doubled. Audibly grainier on broadband material, noticeably cheaper per band.
  • Exaggerated, coarser: --ath-scale 2048. Thresholds eight times stock. The encoder believes almost everything is masked, and the result is a caricature of aggressive coding: hollow, hissy, with detail gone wherever anything louder sits nearby.

Works with / against: --minsnr-scale is the stronger tool for the same goal and partly overrides the effect of this one, since the SNR demand is a floor on quality that a raised threshold cannot push through. --reduce-clamp 0 lets thresholds be pushed deeper than the 29 dB ceiling, which gives low values here more room to act. --spread-mask is the companion knob for the width rather than the height of masking.

--spread-mask <n>

Scales how far masking leaks from one band into its neighbours, which decides how many quiet bands next to loud ones survive.

  • Applies to: every profile with an AAC core. Largest effect where bits are tight but not desperate, roughly 96..192 kbps.
  • Values: Q8, 0 and up, where 256 is x1.0; default: -1, off
  • Input: whole number

A loud tone does not only mask noise in its own band, it masks a skirt of bands around it, more above than below. The model simulates that with a spreading function. The spread is the reason a quiet band sitting next to a loud one gets almost no bits: the model says its content is covered by the neighbour.

This switch scales the spreading. Below 256 means less masking spread, so the skirt shrinks, fewer bands count as covered, and more of them get coded. That is the same idea as relaxing the tone masks noise assumption in other encoders. Above 256 widens the skirt and more bands are written off as covered by their neighbours, which is cheaper and flatter.

The reason the effect concentrates in the 96..192 kbps region is simple arithmetic. Far below that, the bands next to loud ones are not the problem, the whole frame is starved and the rate loop is cutting everywhere. Far above it there are enough bits that the spread rarely decides anything. In between, the spread is what the encoder is actually using to choose what to skip.

Examples:

  • Mild, less masking: --spread-mask 230. A slightly narrower skirt. A few more bands survive beside loud content; more detail around strong partials.
  • Strong, less masking: --spread-mask 180. Clearly more bands coded next to loud ones. On dense material this is where detail around transient energy is regained, at real bit cost.
  • Exaggerated, less masking: --spread-mask 32. Spreading almost removed. The model loses most of its ability to decide what is covered, so bits scatter across bands that genuinely are inaudible; a caricature that shows what the spreading function buys you.
  • Mild, more masking: --spread-mask 300. A touch wider skirt, a small saving.
  • Strong, more masking: --spread-mask 512. Spread doubled. Noticeably flatter spectrum beside loud content, cheap, with quiet detail written off.
  • Exaggerated, more masking: --spread-mask 1536. Almost everything near anything loud counts as masked. The result is hollow and smeared, and useful mainly as the opposite end of the demonstration.

Works with / against: combine with --ath-scale below 256 when you want both a lower and a narrower mask; the two are independent and the effects add. --minsnr-scale still bounds the result from below, so a very low spread cannot push any band below its SNR demand.

--minsnr-scale <n>

Scales the signal to noise ratio the coder is required to deliver in each band; the most direct knob for detail in this group.

  • Applies to: every profile with an AAC core.
  • Values: Q8, 1 and up with no documented ceiling, where 256 is x1.0; default: -1, off
  • Input: whole number

Besides the masking threshold, each band carries a required coding SNR. It is the guaranteed minimum: whatever the threshold computation says, the coder must reach this quality in that band. FDK derives it from its tuning tables, and it is the nearest equivalent to the tone masking noise and noise masking tone constants that MusePack exposes directly.

Below 256 demands a higher SNR than stock, so more detail and more bits per band. Above 256 demands less and the result is coarser. Note the inversion against your intuition: you are scaling the permitted noise, not the demanded quality, so smaller is cleaner.

This switch is more effective than --ath-scale for the same purpose, and the reason is structural. The hole avoidance logic clamps thresholds back to the minimum SNR line, which means the SNR demand is the quality floor the rate loop is not allowed to breach. Move the threshold and the clamp can undo you. Move the demand and the clamp follows.

Examples:

  • Mild, more detail: --minsnr-scale 220. A modest lift in required quality per band. Visible at medium bitrate as fewer bands dropping out.
  • Strong, more detail: --minsnr-scale 150. Clearly higher demand. Combined with a generous bitrate, this is the knob that turns surplus bits into preserved detail; at a tight bitrate it forces the rate loop to make the cut somewhere else.
  • Exaggerated, more detail: --minsnr-scale 16. A sixteen fold demand. The frame can never satisfy it and the rate loop spends the whole frame in rescue mode, so the result is worse than stock; instructive once, not a setting.
  • Mild, coarser: --minsnr-scale 300. A little less demanded per band, a small saving.
  • Strong, coarser: --minsnr-scale 512. Half the demanded quality. Audibly grainier, with a real bit saving that can be redirected with other switches.
  • Exaggerated, coarser: --minsnr-scale 4096. The demand is effectively removed, the floor under quality is gone, and bands collapse freely. A caricature of what the SNR demand was protecting.

Works with / against: --minsnr-clamp-hi and --minsnr-clamp-lo bound the result of this scaling, so pushing far below 256 eventually hits the stock ceiling of about -1 dB unless you raise it with --minsnr-clamp-hi. --reduce-clamp 0 is the usual companion for extreme detail, since it removes the 29 dB reduction ceiling that otherwise limits how deep thresholds can be pushed. It is stronger than --ath-scale, so set this one first and use --ath-scale as a trim.

--minsnr-clamp-hi <n>

Raises or lowers the ceiling on how much SNR a band is allowed to demand.

  • Applies to: every profile with an AAC core. Only acts when some band actually wants more than the stock ceiling, which in practice means you are also using --minsnr-scale below 256.
  • Values: Q8, 1 and up, where 256 is x1.0; default: -1, off
  • Input: whole number

The per band SNR demand is held between a ceiling and a floor. The ceiling, FDK's MAX_SNR, sits at roughly -1 dB and exists so no band can demand an absurd amount of precision and starve the rest of the frame. If you scale the demand up with --minsnr-scale, bands pile up against that ceiling and the scaling stops producing any further effect.

Above 256 here lifts the ceiling, so bands may demand more than stock allows. That is the only way to make very low --minsnr-scale values keep working. Below 256 lowers the ceiling, capping the demand sooner, which is a blunt way to limit how much any one band can take from the frame.

Examples:

  • Mild, higher ceiling: --minsnr-clamp-hi 320. A small amount of headroom above the stock cap. Lets a modest --minsnr-scale keep acting in the most demanding bands.
  • Strong, higher ceiling: --minsnr-clamp-hi 512. Twice the stock cap. This is the pairing for serious detail work at high bitrate.
  • Exaggerated, higher ceiling: --minsnr-clamp-hi 4096. The ceiling is effectively gone and one band can demand everything the frame has. Expect the rate loop to spend its entire budget on a few bands and collapse the rest.
  • Mild, lower ceiling: --minsnr-clamp-hi 220. Demands capped a little sooner; a slight evening out of bit distribution.
  • Strong, lower ceiling: --minsnr-clamp-hi 128. Half the stock cap. No band may ask for much, so the spectrum is coded more uniformly and more coarsely at the peaks.
  • Exaggerated, lower ceiling: --minsnr-clamp-hi 8. The ceiling is so low that the demand is essentially removed as a mechanism, which is the same end state as removing it from below.

Works with / against: only meaningful together with --minsnr-scale; alone it changes nothing unless stock demands were already at the cap. It is the counterpart of --minsnr-clamp-lo, which moves the other end of the same bracket.

Measured: at AAC-LC 64 kbps, --minsnr-clamp-hi 512 changes the stream.

--minsnr-clamp-lo <n>

Raises or lowers the floor under the per band SNR demand, the level below which no band may be allowed to fall.

  • Applies to: every profile with an AAC core, but only where bands are actually being pushed down to the floor, which means very low bitrate. At comfortable bitrates the floor is never reached and the switch does nothing.
  • Values: Q8, 1 and up, where 256 is x1.0; default: -1, off
  • Input: whole number

The other end of the bracket from --minsnr-clamp-hi. FDK's MIN_SNR floor sits at roughly -25 dB and is the worst quality any band may be assigned. Without it, a band under bit pressure could be allocated essentially nothing while still nominally being coded.

Below 256 lowers the floor and lets bands be driven further down, which frees bits for the rest of the frame at the cost of the worst bands getting worse. Above 256 raises the floor, so nothing may get as bad as stock permits, which protects the weakest bands and takes those bits from elsewhere.

Honest status: at ordinary bitrates this switch has no observable effect, because the floor is simply not reached. It is a tool for the 16..32 kbps region and for experiments where you have deliberately starved the core, for instance with a wide --core-cutoff at a low bitrate.

Examples:

  • Mild, higher floor: --minsnr-clamp-lo 300. The worst bands are held slightly better. Only visible at very low bitrate.
  • Strong, higher floor: --minsnr-clamp-lo 512. Twice the stock floor. At low bitrate this reduces the gap between the best and worst coded bands.
  • Exaggerated, higher floor: --minsnr-clamp-lo 2048. The floor sits so high it meets the ceiling and the bracket collapses; every band demands the same, which is not a psychoacoustic model any more.
  • Mild, lower floor: --minsnr-clamp-lo 220. The worst bands may go a little further down, a small amount of bits freed.
  • Strong, lower floor: --minsnr-clamp-lo 128. Half the stock floor. Audible at low bitrate as the quietest bands giving up earlier in exchange for a cleaner midrange.
  • Exaggerated, lower floor: --minsnr-clamp-lo 4. No meaningful protection remains; bands under pressure are allocated almost nothing, which is close to letting them become holes without the hole logic being involved.

Works with / against: pairs with --minsnr-clamp-hi as the two ends of one bracket and with --minsnr-scale, which is what moves the demand inside it. Group E's hole switches address the same symptom from the other side, by controlling which bands may be zeroed outright.

Measured: --minsnr-clamp-lo 512 produced no change at AAC-LC 64 kbps, because the floor is never reached there, while --minsnr-clamp-hi 512 did change the stream at the same settings. Expect this one to need a much lower bitrate before it acts.

--reduce-clamp <0|1>

Removes the 29 dB ceiling on how far the CBR quantiser may push thresholds down, letting the other detail switches act without hitting a wall.

  • Applies to: CBR, that is the default -m 0 path. VBR uses a different code path and is unaffected.
  • Values: 0 or 1; default: 1, the ceiling applies, as in stock
  • Input: whole number, on/off

When the CBR rate loop needs more quality in a band, it reduces that band's threshold. There is a ceiling on the total reduction, about 29 dB, which stops the loop from chasing one demanding band indefinitely. For normal material the ceiling never binds. If you are deliberately demanding much more precision than stock, with --minsnr-scale well below 256 or --ath-scale well below 256, the ceiling is exactly what stops your setting from producing a further effect.

Setting 0 drops the ceiling, so thresholds may be pushed deeper and more bits flow into the demanding bands. On its own this changes little, because stock rarely reaches 29 dB of reduction. It is a companion switch, not a standalone one.

Values:

  • 1: the 29 dB reduction ceiling applies (stock, default).
  • 0: ceiling removed, thresholds may be pushed deeper.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 2 -b 256000 --minsnr-scale 150 --reduce-clamp 0 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: pair it with --minsnr-scale below 256 for extreme detail; it is near pointless alone. No effect under VBR (-m 1..5).

--tns-mask <n>

Chooses which of the temporal noise shaping filters the encoder may use.

  • Applies to: every profile with an AAC core. It matters most in LD and ELD, where TNS is the only defence against pre-echo, and on transient heavy material in LC.
  • Values: 0..15, a four bit mask; default: -1, stock 0xF, all filters allowed
  • Input: whole number

TNS shapes quantisation noise in time so it hides under an attack instead of sitting in the quiet before it. FDK runs more than one TNS filter, differing by block type and by the frequency region they cover, and the mask says which of them are enabled. Stock is 0xF, which is 15, meaning all four bits set.

This is a diagnostic and laboratory switch rather than a quality knob. The useful settings are 0, which disables TNS entirely and lets you hear what it was doing, and 15, which is stock. Intermediate masks let you isolate one filter, which is interesting when you are trying to attribute an artifact, and rarely interesting otherwise.

Disabling TNS has a second consequence worth knowing: FDK forces PNS off when TNS is off. So --tns-mask 0 silently takes perceptual noise substitution with it.

Values:

  • -1: stock, all filters enabled (0xF).
  • 0: TNS fully off. Pre-echo on sharp attacks gets noticeably worse, especially in LD and ELD; PNS is forced off as well.
  • 1 to 14: individual filters enabled by bit. For isolating one filter's contribution.
  • 15: all filters enabled, the same as stock.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 2 -b 128000 --tns-mask 0 --verbose -o no_tns.m4a attacks.wav

Works with / against: --tns-order limits how long the enabled filters may be, so the two are read together. --tns-thresh in group E decides how often TNS switches on at all, which is the knob you usually want instead of the mask. Setting 0 here forces PNS off regardless of --pns. --verbose prints the effective TNS mask as a hex bitmask.

--tns-order <n>

Caps the length of the temporal noise shaping filter, trading how precisely the noise envelope can follow the signal against the side information it costs.

  • Applies to: every profile with an AAC core. Short blocks are additionally capped at order 5 by the standard, so values above that only affect long blocks.
  • Values: 1..12; default: -1, FDK's own order
  • Input: whole number

The TNS filter is a linear predictor running across frequency. Its order is how many coefficients it has, and the order determines how detailed a time envelope it can impose on the noise. A low order can only produce a crude shape, roughly "more noise later, less earlier". A high order can follow a complicated envelope, at the cost of sending more filter coefficients per frame as side information.

Lower values make TNS cheaper and blunter. At low bitrate that trade can be worth it, because the coefficients compete with the spectrum for the same bits. Higher values let the shaping track attacks more precisely, which is the direction to go when pre-echo is the problem and you have bits, particularly in LD and ELD where no short blocks exist to help.

Examples:

  • Mild, longer filter: --tns-order 8. Slightly finer time shaping in long blocks, a small side information cost.
  • Strong, longer filter: --tns-order 12. The maximum. Best time resolution TNS can give; worth trying in LD and ELD on percussive material.
  • Exaggerated, longer filter: --tns-order 12 is already the ceiling, so the caricature here is combining it with --tns-thresh 0.5 from group E so the long filter also runs on nearly every frame. Expect side information to eat a visible share of a low bitrate budget and holes to appear in exchange.
  • Mild, shorter filter: --tns-order 5. The short block limit applied everywhere. Cheaper, with slightly coarser shaping in long blocks.
  • Strong, shorter filter: --tns-order 3. Crude envelope shaping only. Cheap; most of the pre-echo benefit is gone but the gross shaping remains.
  • Exaggerated, shorter filter: --tns-order 1. A single coefficient, which can express almost nothing about the envelope. TNS is nominally on and effectively useless, which is a good way to confirm how much of the benefit comes from the filter length rather than from TNS being enabled.

Works with / against: --tns-mask decides which filters exist, this decides how long they are; 0 on the mask makes this irrelevant. --tns-thresh in group E decides how often they run. In LC, short blocks already address most transients, so the order matters less there than in LD and ELD.

--pns <n>

Turns perceptual noise substitution on or off, overriding the encoder's own decision.

  • Applies to: AAC-LC, AAC-LD and AAC-ELD cores under CBR. FDK forces PNS off when the bitrate mode is VBR and when TNS is off, and those rules are above this switch.
  • Values: 0 off, 1 on; default: -1, the encoder decides
  • Input: whole number, on/off

PNS replaces a band judged to be noise with a single energy value and a flag, and the decoder regenerates noise at that energy. On noisy material the saving is large, and on tonal material the risk is that a real tone becomes hiss.

The encoder's own decision comes from its tuning table and depends mostly on bitrate. At 64 kbps PNS is normally already on, so 1 there is the same as stock and only 0 changes anything. Below about 28 kbps the table disables PNS completely, and at that point neither 1 here nor --pns-start does anything, because PNS is switched off higher in the chain; --force-pns is the switch that bypasses that gate.

The honest use for this card is diagnosis and deliberate choice: 0 to hear what PNS was contributing, 1 to assert it where you believe the table is being conservative. --verbose reports the resulting PNS state, which is the quickest way to find out whether your request survived the gates.

Values:

  • -1: leave FDK's decision (stock, default).
  • 0: PNS off. On noisy material expect the noise bands to be coded properly and the bits to come from somewhere else.
  • 1: PNS on, where the gates permit. At 64 kbps and up this usually matches stock; at low bitrate it needs --force-pns to have any effect.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 2 -b 64000 --pns 0 --verbose -o no_pns.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: --force-pns removes the low bitrate gate. --tns-mask 0 forces PNS off whatever you set here, because FDK requires TNS for PNS. VBR mode (-m 1..5) does the same. --pns-start, --pns-tonality, --pns-refpower, --pns-gapfill and --pns-min-width all shape PNS once it is running and do nothing when it is not.

Measured: at AAC-LC 64 kbps, --pns 1 gives the stock stream, since stock already has PNS on there, while --pns 0 changes it.

--pns-start <hz>

Sets the lowest frequency at which noise substitution is allowed to act.

  • Applies to: the same places as --pns, and only when PNS is actually running.
  • Values: frequency in Hz; default: -1, FDK's own start frequency
  • Input: whole number

PNS is safe high up and dangerous low down. Low bands carry the fundamental tones that define pitch and timbre, and substituting noise there is immediately audible. FDK therefore has a start frequency below which PNS is never used, and it depends on bitrate and sample rate.

Lower the start and more of the spectrum becomes eligible, so more bands get noise coded and more bits are freed, with a growing risk of substituting something that was not noise. Raise it and PNS retreats upward, which is safer and cheaper in savings.

One practical trap: the value is snapped to a scalefactor band boundary, so a change of a few hundred Hz may land in the same band and produce an identical stream. Move in steps of a kilohertz or more when you are probing.

Examples:

  • Mild, lower start: --pns-start 3000. PNS reaches a little deeper. Slightly more bands noise coded on broadband material.
  • Strong, lower start: --pns-start 2000. Clearly more of the spectrum eligible. A real saving on noisy material; on anything with upper midrange tonality, listen for the tone turning grainy.
  • Exaggerated, lower start: --pns-start 500. PNS allowed almost everywhere. Sustained notes lose their identity where the detector misfires, which is exactly the artifact the start frequency exists to prevent.
  • Mild, higher start: --pns-start 6000. PNS pushed up a bit, a small loss of saving and a small gain in safety.
  • Strong, higher start: --pns-start 12000. Only the top of the spectrum may be substituted. Safe, with most of the benefit gone.
  • Exaggerated, higher start: --pns-start 20000. Above the coded band in most configurations, so PNS has nowhere left to act and this is effectively --pns 0 by another route.

Works with / against: does nothing when PNS is off, which at low bitrate means you need --force-pns first. --pns-min-width restricts PNS by band width rather than by frequency, and since bands get wider as frequency rises, the two restrictions overlap. --verbose shows the resulting PNS state.

Measured: at AAC-LC 64 kbps and 48 kHz the stock start is 4100 Hz. 4000 maps to the same scalefactor band and gives an identical stream; 2000 and 12000 both change it.

--force-pns

Bypasses the low bitrate gate that otherwise disables noise substitution entirely below about 28 kbps.

  • Applies to: AAC-LC, AAC-LD and AAC-ELD under CBR, at bitrates below the gate. Above the gate it changes nothing, because PNS is already permitted.
  • Values: flag; default: off
  • Input: flag

FDK's tuning table has a bitrate row below roughly 28 kbps in which every PNS entry is zero, for every sample rate. The consequence surprises people: at 24 kbps, exactly where cheap noise coding would help most, PNS is completely off, and both --pns 1 and --pns-start are ignored. The audible result is the MDCT style artifact on noisy content that you would otherwise associate with MP3.

This flag makes the encoder use the first active row of the table instead of the zero row, so PNS also works at 24 kbps and below. The rest of FDK's requirements still apply: PNS needs TNS enabled and a non VBR mode, and nothing in this build changes that.

Values:

  • omitted: the gate applies, PNS off below about 28 kbps (stock).
  • --force-pns: gate bypassed, PNS available at low bitrate.

Example in context:

fdkaac -p 2 -b 24000 --pns 1 --force-pns --pns-start 4000 -o out.m4a in.wav

Works with / against: usually given together with --pns 1 and often with --pns-start, since the point of forcing PNS is to then control it. Pointless with --tns-mask 0 or under VBR, both of which force PNS off anyway. At bitrates above the gate it is a no-op.

--pns-shared <0|1>

In bands that both channels code as noise, write one shared noise or two independent ones, the same way in every band.

  • Applies to: stereo AAC-LC core with PNS on (--pns 1, --force-pns or --pns-above). Off by default.
  • Values: 1 = one shared noise (the same noise in L and R), 0 = independent noise in each channel

PNS replaces a band by noise of the right level. In a channel pair the format can say, band by band, that the decoder should use the same noise in both channels. That flag rides on the MS mask. Stock decides it per band by measuring the correlation, so the mask usually has to be written band by band: up to about 25 bits per frame at low rates. A uniform choice lets the mask fall back to its 2-bit all-bands form when the rest of the frame allows it. The noise level is still written for each channel: the format has no shared level.

Examples (stereo music, 24 kbps, PNS from 2.2 kHz):

  • Typical: --pns-shared 1. MS mask 28 to 2 bits per frame on correlated material.
  • Wide stereo: --pns-shared 0. Independent noise keeps the width, the mask stays as expensive as stock.

When the mask still stays per band (bands below the PNS region coded as L/R on wide material) the switch saves little: the mask is only cheap when every band agrees. ffmpeg 6.1.1 plays shared noise as independent noise; the FDK decoder plays it as shared.

--pns-hold <dB>

Let neighbouring noise bands share one level, so the noise envelope costs fewer bits.

  • Applies to: AAC core with PNS on. Off by default.
  • Values: 0.1..12.0 dB

Each PNS band carries its noise level as a difference from the previous one: 1 bit when it is the same, 3 to 9 bits otherwise. With this switch a band whose level is within the given tolerance of the previous noise band takes that level. The noise envelope over frequency gets coarser, in steps of about 1.5 dB.

Examples (stereo music, 24 kbps, PNS from 2.2 kHz):

  • Mild: --pns-hold 3. Noise levels 94 to 87 bits per frame.
  • Strong: --pns-hold 6. 70 bits per frame; the freed bits go to the coded spectrum.
  • Exaggerated: --pns-hold 12. The noise is almost flat over its whole region.

--pns-short <0|1>

Use --pns-above also in short (transient) frames, where it pays off.

  • Applies to: AAC-LC core with --pns-above. Off by default.
  • Values: 1 = on, 0 = stock

The AAC format allows noise substitution in short windows; FDK never does it, so above --pns-above a transient frame codes the noise bands as spectrum. With this switch they become noise too, but only in a frame coded as one group of all 8 windows (--short-groups 1). Measured (1.4.0-beta.2) in smaller groups: the noise energies took 19% of the frame instead of 2%, and the attack came out 2.6..6 dB quieter, so those groups keep the spectrum. With the stock grouping the switch therefore changes nothing (bit for bit).

Measured with --short-groups 1 --pns-above 4000, a sample with an attack in every frame: 24 kbps file 9810 to 9226 B (-6%), the noise band at the source level (+1.1 dB instead of -1.4 dB). The cost: one noise level covers all 8 windows, so the band is smeared over the frame: pre-echo +1 dB, attack about 2 dB quieter. On music with few short frames it does nearly nothing (-0.3% of the file). For low bitrates where the band would otherwise be missing; listen first.

--pns-budget <0|1>

Count the bits the PNS noise levels take, so a frame does not run out of bits and cut its spectrum.

  • Applies to: AAC core with PNS on. Off by default.
  • Values: 1 = on, 0 = stock

FDK treats a PNS band as free when it plans the bits of a frame, but every noise level costs bits in the stream. At low bitrates with a lot of PNS the frame then overflows, and the emergency cut removes bands from the top. Stock gives the cut no credit for the PNS bands, so it goes on through the noise region into the coded spectrum below it: whole frames lose everything above about 1.5 kHz, which sounds like a crackle in frame rhythm. With this switch the bit target for the spectrum is lowered by the cost of the noise levels, and the cut gets those bits back when it removes a noise band.

Measured on acoustic stereo music, -b 24000 --block-bias 0 -w 5500 --pns-above 2200: frames hit by the emergency cut 42 of 766 to 1, frame-rhythm modulation of the noise band 19.7 to 11.0 dB (11.6 without PNS). Together with --pns-shared 1: 0 frames cut, 8.3 dB.

Recommended for any forced PNS at low bitrate: --pns-budget 1 --pns-shared 1, optionally --pns-hold 3.

--pns-gain <x>

Sets how loud the fabricated noise is relative to the energy of the band it replaced.

  • Applies to: wherever PNS is actually running.
  • Values: 0.0 and up, where 1.0 leaves the energy unchanged; default: -1, off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

When PNS substitutes a band, the one number it stores is that band's noise energy, and the decoder regenerates random noise scaled to it. This switch scales the stored energy directly, so it is the "how loud is the substituted noise" dial and the PNS knob you will reach for most often.

At 1.0 the regenerated noise carries the original band's energy, which is what the mechanism intends. Above 1.0 the fill is louder than the original, which helps when the substituted noise sounds too timid and the mix turns dull: real noise floors often feel quieter after substitution because the regenerated noise lacks the original's structure. Below 1.0 it is quieter, which is the move when the fill hisses more than the source did.

This is pure taste territory with no correct value, which is why both directions are worth trying on your own material before settling.

Examples:

  • Mild, louder: --pns-gain 1.2. Slightly fuller noise fill; useful when cymbal wash loses presence.
  • Strong, louder: --pns-gain 2.0. Twice the energy. Clearly more hiss and air; on broadband noise this can sound closer to the source than the nominally correct value.
  • Exaggerated, louder: --pns-gain 8.0. Eight times the energy. The substituted bands dominate the top of the mix with obvious artificial hiss, which is a quick way to hear exactly which bands PNS has taken.
  • Mild, quieter: --pns-gain 0.8. A slightly more discreet fill.
  • Strong, quieter: --pns-gain 0.4. Noise fill clearly below the original energy. The top sounds cleaner and also duller, and a noise floor can disappear.
  • Exaggerated, quieter: --pns-gain 0.0. Substituted bands carry no energy at all, which means they are silent, so PNS becomes a hole generator. A caricature, and a useful demonstration of how much of the signal was being carried by noise bands.

Works with / against: does nothing when PNS is off. --pns-tonality, --pns-refpower, --pns-gapfill and --pns-min-width decide which bands get substituted; this one only changes how loud the result is. Using it at 0.0 is a clumsier way to get what --pns 0 gives properly.

--pns-tonality <x>

Scales the tonality threshold that decides whether a band counts as noise and may be substituted.

  • Applies to: wherever PNS is actually running.
  • Values: 0.0 and up, where 1.0 is the factory threshold; default: -1, off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

PNS only fires on bands the encoder judges noise like, which it measures as low tonality. The threshold is the line between "tonal, code it properly" and "noise, substitute it". This switch scales that line.

Higher than 1.0 lets more bands qualify, including somewhat tonal ones, so the noise substitution gets wider: more of the spectrum replaced by a cheap description, more saving, and more risk that a genuine tone gets smeared into hiss. Lower than 1.0 makes the detector pickier, so only the clearly noisy bands are substituted, which is safer and saves less.

This is the knob that controls the breadth of PNS, as opposed to --pns-start, which controls where it is geographically allowed, and --pns-min-width, which controls it by band size.

Examples:

  • Mild, wider substitution: --pns-tonality 1.3. A few more borderline bands become PNS. Small saving, little risk.
  • Strong, wider substitution: --pns-tonality 2.0. Twice the threshold. Clearly more bands substituted; on noisy material a real saving, on tonal material audible smearing of partials.
  • Exaggerated, wider substitution: --pns-tonality 20.0. Almost everything above the start frequency counts as noise. Sustained tones turn to hiss and the top of the mix becomes a noise bed: a caricature that makes the mechanism unmistakable.
  • Mild, narrower substitution: --pns-tonality 0.8. Slightly pickier detection.
  • Strong, narrower substitution: --pns-tonality 0.4. Only unambiguous noise is substituted. Safe, with much of the saving gone.
  • Exaggerated, narrower substitution: --pns-tonality 0.01. Effectively nothing qualifies, so this reaches the same place as --pns 0 while leaving PNS nominally enabled.

Works with / against: --pns-refpower is the other half of the detection decision and both must agree before a band becomes PNS, so moving only this one can be blocked by the power gate. --pns-gain changes the loudness of the result, not the selection. --pns-min-width can exclude a band that passed this test.

--pns-refpower <x>

Scales the reference power threshold a band's level must satisfy before it is considered a noise candidate.

  • Applies to: wherever PNS is actually running.
  • Values: 0.0 and up, where 1.0 is the factory threshold; default: -1, off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

Tonality is not the only test. Each band's power is also compared against a reference before the band can be a PNS candidate, which keeps the mechanism away from bands whose level makes substitution a bad trade. This switch moves that power gate.

The two detection tests are conjunctive: tonality and reference power must both agree before a band becomes PNS. That is why moving --pns-tonality alone sometimes does nothing, and why this card and that one are usually tried together.

The value multiplies the threshold itself. Higher than 1.0 raises it, so fewer bands pass and PNS retreats; lower than 1.0 lowers it, so more bands pass. Measured on acoustic stereo music at 64 kbps LC, PNS bands per frame: 5.5 at 0.01, 5.1 at 0.4, 4.8 at 0.8, 4.6 at 1.0, 4.3 at 1.3, 3.6 at 2.0, 0 at 20.0.

Examples:

  • Mild, looser gate: --pns-refpower 0.8. Slightly more candidates (4.6 to 4.8 bands).
  • Strong, looser gate: --pns-refpower 0.4. 5.1 bands. Combined with a raised --pns-tonality this is where wide noise substitution takes hold.
  • Exaggerated, looser gate: --pns-refpower 0.01. The power test stops discriminating and selection is left to tonality (5.5 bands), a good way to find out how much work this gate was doing.
  • Mild, stricter gate: --pns-refpower 1.3. Slightly fewer candidates (4.3 bands).
  • Strong, stricter gate: --pns-refpower 2.0. Twice the threshold, 3.6 bands. PNS retreats to the bands where it is least risky.
  • Exaggerated, stricter gate: --pns-refpower 20.0. Nothing passes, so PNS is on paper enabled and in practice idle.

Works with / against: conjunctive with --pns-tonality, so test them as a pair. --pns-gapfill can still add a band between two PNS bands that failed this test. Nothing here matters when PNS is off.

--pns-gapfill <x>

Scales the threshold of the heuristic that closes a one band gap between two substituted bands.

  • Applies to: wherever PNS is running, and only when two PNS bands already sit on either side of a coded band. Honest status: wired, no effect observed on our test material.
  • Values: 0.0 and up, where 1.0 is the factory threshold; default: -1, off
  • Input: decimal, up to 3 places, . or ,

When PNS has marked bands on both sides of a narrow gap, a coded island stranded between two noise bands is usually worse than extending the noise across it: the island is the only place with real spectral structure and it stands out. The gap fill heuristic can mark that band as PNS too, and this switch scales the threshold it uses.

Higher makes gap filling more willing, lower makes it reluctant. Both directions are subtle at best, because the precondition is strict: for AAC-LC the rule needs both neighbours to be PNS already, and on our material that configuration did not arise, so no value of this switch changed the output. The switch is present and wired, and it may act on material whose spectrum produces the required pattern, but do not expect it to be a tool you reach for.

If you want a genuine lever on how much of the spectrum becomes noise, use --pns-tonality or --pns-start instead.

Examples:

  • Mild, more filling: --pns-gapfill 1.3. Slightly more willing to close a gap.
  • Strong, more filling: --pns-gapfill 3.0. Clearly more willing, where the precondition occurs at all.
  • Exaggerated, more filling: --pns-gapfill 50.0. Any gap between two PNS bands is closed. On material that triggers it, this produces contiguous noise regions where the encoder had kept an island.
  • Mild, less filling: --pns-gapfill 0.7. Slightly more reluctant.
  • Strong, less filling: --pns-gapfill 0.3. Gaps are left as coded islands.
  • Exaggerated, less filling: --pns-gapfill 0.0. Gap filling never happens, which is the closest thing to switching the heuristic off.

Works with / against: depends entirely on --pns-tonality and --pns-refpower producing two adjacent PNS bands with a gap, so widening those first is the only way to give this switch something to act on.

Measured: no stream change across 4 test signals, 5 bitrates and 6 values of this switch in AAC-LC. The precondition for the AAC-LC gap fill rule, both neighbours already PNS, did not occur on that material.

--pns-min-width <n>

Sets the minimum scalefactor band width, in spectral lines, that a band must have before noise substitution may act on it.

  • Applies to: wherever PNS is running. Only values above the built-in default have any effect, which for AAC-LC means above 16.
  • Values: raw line count, 1 and up; default: -1, off, which means FDK's own minimum (16 for AAC-LC)
  • Input: whole number

Scalefactor bands get wider as frequency rises. A narrow band at the bottom of the spectrum holds few lines and usually carries tonal content, and substituting noise there is conspicuous. A wide band high up holds many lines and is where PNS belongs. This switch expresses that preference directly, as a minimum width.

Note the asymmetry, because the obvious reading of the name is wrong. Raising the minimum restricts PNS to wider bands, so to the upper spectrum, and that works. Lowering it below FDK's own minimum does not open PNS up on narrower bands: FDK's default is already the effective floor, so values below it give the stock stream. There is one useful direction here, upward.

Unlike the --pns-* switches above, this is a raw count of lines, not a multiplier.

Examples:

  • Mild, wider bands only: --pns-min-width 24. PNS restricted a little further up the spectrum. Small reduction in substitution, small loss of saving.
  • Strong, wider bands only: --pns-min-width 32. Only clearly wide bands may be substituted. Safer on tonal material, with a visible drop in the PNS saving.
  • Exaggerated, wider bands only: --pns-min-width 64. At AAC-LC 64 kbps this leaves no qualifying band at all, so the result is identical to --pns 0: PNS is enabled and has nothing it may touch.
  • Downward there is no useful example: 2 and 4 both give the stock stream, because the built-in minimum of 16 already binds.

Works with / against: overlaps with --pns-start, which restricts PNS by frequency rather than by width; since width grows with frequency the two restrictions reinforce each other and the tighter one wins. Does nothing when PNS is off.

Measured: at AAC-LC, values 2 and 4 give the stock stream, confirming that the built-in default of 16 is the floor. 64 at AAC-LC 64 kbps produces exactly the stream that --pns 0 produces, so PNS is effectively off at that width.

E. Core transients, pre-echo and spectral holes

These thirteen switches reach the AAC core itself, under SBR. They matter most where the core is short of bits: AAC-LC stereo at 24..50 kbps with the bandwidth forced well above what FDK would pick (-w 16000..20000), and LD or ELD with a widened --core-cutoff. At comfortable bitrates most of them change little, because the problems they address hardly occur there.

Two different failures show up under bit pressure, and it helps to keep them apart.

Pre-echo. A long AAC block covers 2048 samples, about 43 ms at 48 kHz. The quantisation noise of a block is spread over the whole block in time. When a sharp attack sits late in the block, that noise also lands before the attack, where the ear has almost nothing to mask it: backward masking lasts only a few milliseconds. You hear it as a soft smear or a "pre-shadow" in front of castanets, hand claps, plucked strings or a piano hit. The encoder has four tools against it, and this group exposes all four:

  1. the attack detector, which switches the frame to eight short blocks of 256 samples (--attack-decay, --attack-floor, together with --block-bias in group F);
  2. the grouping of those eight short windows into scalefactor groups (--short-groups);
  3. the window shape, which decides how much noise leaks away from where it belongs (--window-shape);
  4. for the cases that stay in long blocks, TNS (temporal noise shaping, which shapes the noise in time inside a block: --tns-thresh, --tns-start) and the pre-echo control, which limits how fast a masking threshold may grow from one frame to the next (--preecho-rise, --preecho-depth).

AAC-LD and AAC-ELD have no short blocks and a fixed window, so for them only TNS and the pre-echo control are left. That is why those switches matter more there than in LC.

Spectral holes. When a frame cannot afford all the bands it is asked to code, FDK first relaxes the SNR it demands from bands that are peaks or valleys of the spectrum, and if that is still not enough, it cuts whole scalefactor bands to zero. A zeroed band is silence in that frequency range for that frame. Over time this sounds like a bubbling, flickering or "underwater" high end: bands come and go from frame to frame. FDK calls the whole mechanism "avoid holes", which is somewhat ironic: it decides which holes to make. The order is fixed: bands are cut in up to eight energy steps, quietest first, and only from a starting band upwards, so that the bottom of the spectrum is protected. The switches --avoid-holes, --hole-start, --hole-start-short, --hole-spread and --hole-ladder change that mechanism.

There is no free lunch in either case. Under CBR the frame budget is fixed. A band that is not cut costs bits that some other band no longer gets, and a short block costs more side information than a long one. Every switch here moves bits around; none adds any.

All measurements quoted in this group were taken after decoding, on two synthetic signals built for the purpose. attacks48.wav holds castanet-like clicks over a very quiet bed; pre-echo is the error energy in the 1024 samples before each attack, relative to the energy of the attack itself. dense48.wav holds dense wideband chords, bright noise and a hi-hat; a hole is a 1/3-octave band between 2 and 16 kHz whose original energy is above -70 dBFS and which comes out of the decoder at least 15 dB too low. The tools that produce these numbers are in tools/core/ in the repository, so the measurements can be repeated on any material.

A starting point for AAC-LC stereo at 24..50 kbps with a wide band:

fdkaac-franken-x64.exe -p 2 -b 32000 -w 17000 --hole-ladder 2 --avoid-holes 1 --window-shape 1 -o out.m4a in.wav

--hole-ladder <1..8>

Limits how far the encoder may go when it cuts bands to zero, so that a starved frame gets coarser instead of emptier.

  • Applies to: the AAC core in every profile (LC, the core of HE-AAC and HE-AAC v2, LD, ELD). Matters only when bits run out.
  • Values: 1..8; default: off, which is stock behaviour (8 steps)
  • Input: whole number

When a frame is over budget, FDK sorts the candidate bands by energy into eight steps and cuts them step by step, quietest first, rechecking the budget after each step. With eight steps, a hard frame can end up cutting quite loud bands. --hole-ladder N stops after N steps. Only the quietest bands may then be cut. If the frame is still over budget, the rate loop solves the rest the other way, by raising the quantisation step in all bands: the result is more noise spread evenly instead of more bands missing.

This is the strongest switch against the bubbling top at low bitrate. The trade is real but usually favourable: a slightly grainier fill in exchange for bands that stay present from frame to frame. The ear is far more tolerant of steady noise than of bands that blink.

Examples (only one direction exists: fewer steps than stock):

  • Mild: --hole-ladder 6. Only the loudest cut steps disappear. Hard frames lose a few fewer bands; easy frames are untouched.
  • Strong: --hole-ladder 2. Only the two quietest steps may be cut. On dense material at 32..50 kbps this removes most holes (see Measured).
